The [People's Republic of China](5405.md) (PRC) has a [socialist market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_market_economy), incorporating [industrial policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_policy) and strategic [five-year plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five-year_plans_of_China).[^cite_note-China_mixed-28] China has the [world's second-largest economy by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) and since 2016 has been the [world's largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_past_and_projected_GDP_\(PPP\)#IMF_estimates_between_2010_and_2019) when measured by [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) (PPP).[^cite_note-29][^cite_note-30] China accounted for 19% of the [global economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_economy) in 2025 in PPP terms,[^cite_note-PWC-31] and around 17% in nominal terms in 2025.[^cite_note-PWC-31][^cite_note-32] The [developing](78449.md) economy consists of [state-owned enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-owned_enterprises_of_China) (SOEs) and mixed-ownership enterprises, as well as a large domestic private sector which contribute approximately 60% of the GDP, 80% of urban employment and 90% of new jobs.

China is the world's [largest manufacturing industrial economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_manufacturing_output) and [exporter of goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_exports). China is widely regarded as the "powerhouse of manufacturing", "the factory of the world" and the world's "manufacturing superpower".[^cite_note-33] Its production exceeds that of the nine next largest manufacturers combined.[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] [Exports as a percentage of GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_trade-to-GDP_ratio) are around 20%. China is the largest trading nation in the world and plays a prominent role in [international trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_trade).[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38] Manufacturing has been transitioning toward high-tech industries such as [electric vehicles](279350.md), [renewable energy](25784.md), [telecommunications](33094374.md) and [IT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_technology) equipment, and services has also grown as a percentage of GDP. China is the world's largest [high technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_technology) exporter.[^cite_note-39] As of 2023, the country spends [around 2.6% of GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_by_research_and_development_spending) to advance [research and development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_development) across various sectors of the economy.[^cite_note-40] It is also the world's [second-largest importer of goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_imports).[^cite_note-41] China is a net importer of services products.[^cite_note-42]

China has a network of [free trade agreements](2533562.md) with [several countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade_agreements_of_China), though [Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_Comprehensive_Economic_Partnership) (RCEP) accounts for the bulk of its trade integration.[^cite_note-43] Of the world's [500 largest companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_Global_500), 142 are headquartered in China.[^cite_note-Global_500-44] It has three of the world's top ten [most competitive financial centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Financial_Centres_Index)[^cite_note-GFCI36-45] and three of the world's ten [largest stock exchanges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_stock_exchanges) (both by [market capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_capitalization) and by [trade volume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_volume)).[^cite_note-ValueWalk_2019-46] China has the [second-largest financial assets in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_by_financial_assets), valued at \$28.3 trillion as of 2024.[^cite_note-47] China is one of the largest recipient of [foreign direct investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment) (FDI) in the world as of 2025, receiving inflows of \$107 billion.[^cite_note-48] It has the third [largest outbound FDI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_FDI_abroad), at US\$192.20 billion for 2024.[^cite_note-49] China's economic growth has dealt with a range of challenges in the 2020s due to a [property crisis](68779824.md).[^cite_note-50]

With 773 million workers, the Chinese [labor force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_force) is [the world's largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_labour_force) as of 2024, although it is shrinking due to the [rapidly aging population](60912277.md).[^cite_note-\:0-51] In March 2026, *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* estimated China ranked second in the world, after the U.S., in [total number of billionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_billionaires) and [total number of millionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_millionaires), with 539 Chinese billionaires,[^cite_note-52] while *Hurun Global Rich List* estimated it ranked first, with 1,110 billionaires.[^cite_note-53] China also has 5.3 million millionaires as of 2026, second highest after the U.S.[^cite_note-54] [Public social expenditure in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_in_China) was around 10% of GDP.[^cite_note-55]

## History

Main articles: [Economic history of China before 1912](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_China_before_1912), [Economic history of China (1912–1949)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_China_\(1912%E2%80%931949\)), and [Economic history of China (1949–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_China_\(1949%E2%80%93present\))



The global contribution to world's GDP by major economies from 1 CE to 2003 CE according to Angus Maddison's estimates.

[^cite_note-56]

&#x20;Up until the 18th century, China and India were the two largest economies by GDP output.



GDP per capita in China from 1850 to 2022

Historically, China was one of the [world's foremost economic powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_regions_by_past_GDP_\(PPP\)) for most of the two millennia of *[Pax Sinica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pax_Sinica)*, spanning from [the 1st until the 19th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_China_before_1912).[^cite_note-Maddison_2008-57][^cite_note-58] China accounted for around one-quarter to one-third of global GDP until the mid-1800s. China's share of global GDP was one-third in 1820 as the Industrial Revolution was beginning in Great Britain.[^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62] China's GDP in 1820 was six times as large as Britain's, the largest economy in Europe, and almost twenty times the GDP of the nascent United States.[^cite_note-63]

At the end of the [Chinese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Civil_War), the economy was devastated.[^cite_note-Karl_2010-64] As the defeated [Kuomintang retreated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retreat_of_the_government_of_the_Republic_of_China_to_Taiwan) to Taiwan, they stripped mainland China of liquid assets, including gold, silver, and the country's [dollar reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign-exchange_reserves_of_China).[^cite_note-Karl_2010-64] By the time the Kuomintang (KMT) was defeated, commerce had been destroyed, the national currency rendered valueless, and the economy reduced to barter.[^cite_note-Karl_2010-64]

The development of the People's Republic of China (PRC) from one of the poorest countries to one of the largest economies was the quickest of any country.[^cite_note-65]<sup>: 11 </sup> From 1949 until the [reform and opening up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up) in 1978, the country maintained a self-sufficient, state-led economy focused on rapid industrialization, while substantial market activity remained underground.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-Frost_2023-67] The period of the [Great Leap Forward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Leap_Forward) and the [Great Chinese Famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Chinese_Famine) negatively impacted the economy.[^cite_note-68] Economic reforms began under [Deng Xiaoping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping).[^cite_note-Frost_2023-67] China subsequently became the world's [fastest-growing major economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_real_GDP_growth_rate), with [growth rates averaging 10%](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_GDP_of_China) over 30 years.[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70] Many scholars consider the [Chinese economic model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Consensus) as an example of [authoritarian capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian_capitalism),[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72] [state capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_capitalism),[^cite_note-73] or [party-state capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party-state_capitalism) under the [general secretaryship of Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_secretaryship_of_Xi_Jinping).[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75] At the same time, scholars have also emphasized differences across reform-era leaderships, especially between the Deng Xiaoping and Xi Jinping era. Another inflection point is the COVID-19 pandemic, which had an enduring impact on the Chinese economy.[^cite_note-76] Yuen Yuen Ang characterizes the post-pandemic period as "China's economic paradox:" a period of rapid technological catch-up paired with a broad growth slowdown.[^cite_note-77]

China brought more people out of extreme [poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_China) than any other country in history.[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79] Between 1978 and 2018, China reduced extreme poverty by 800 million people.[^cite_note-80] The percentage of the population living in [extreme poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_poverty) decreased from 88.1% to 0.2% between 1981 and 2019.[^cite_note-POVCAL-81] Its [current account surplus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Current_account_surplus) increased by a factor of 53 between 1982 and 2021, growing from \$5.67 billion to \$317 billion.[^cite_note-82]

During this time, China became an industrial powerhouse, moving beyond initial successes in low-wage sectors like [clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clothing) and [footwear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Footwear) to the sophisticated production of [computers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computers), [pharmaceuticals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceuticals), and [automobiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automobiles). China's factories generated \$3.7 trillion in real manufacturing value added, more than the US, South Korea, Germany, the UK and France combined. China's manufacturing sector benefits from one of the world's largest domestic markets, immense manufacturing scale, and highly developed manufacturing supply chains.[^cite_note-83] It also has two of the global top 5 science and technology clusters—[Shenzhen-Hong Kong-Guangzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangdong%E2%80%93Hong_Kong%E2%80%93Macau_Greater_Bay_Area) and [Beijing](18603746.md), in the 2nd and 3rd spots respectively—which is more than any other country.[^cite_note-Dutta_2022-84][^cite_note-85]

China has sustained growth due to export relations, its [manufacturing sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing_sector), and low-wage workers.[^cite_note-CIAWFCH-86] It was the only major world economy to experience GDP expansion in 2020, with a growth rate of 2.3%.[^cite_note-Marquis_2022-87] However, the country posted one of its worst economic performances in decades in 2022 due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_China).[^cite_note-88] In 2023, the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) (IMF) predicted that China would continue to be one of the fastest growing major economies.[^cite_note-89] China's economy is both a contributor to rising global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and severely affected by the [adverse impacts of climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impacts_of_climate_change), although its per capita emissions remain much lower than developed economies like the United States.[^cite_note-World_Bank-90]

## Regional economies

See also: [Megalopolises in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalopolises_in_China) and [List of cities in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_China)



Distribution of GDP in mainland China

China's unequal [transportation system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_China), combined with important differences in the availability of [natural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_resource) and [human resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_resources) and in industrial [infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure), has produced significant variations in the regional economies of China.[^cite_note-91] The economic development of [Shenzhen](83368.md) has caused the city to be referred to as the world's next [Silicon Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_Valley).[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93]

[Economic development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_development) has generally been more rapid in [coastal provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_places_on_the_coastline_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) than in the interior and there are large disparities in [per capita income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita_income) between regions. The three wealthiest regions are the [Yangtze Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yangtze_Delta) in East China; the [Pearl River Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearl_River_Delta) in South China; and [Jing-Jin-Ji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jing-Jin-Ji) region in North China. It is the rapid development of these areas that is expected to have the most significant effect on the Asian regional economy as a whole and Chinese government policy is designed to remove the obstacles to accelerated growth in these wealthier regions. By 2035, China's four cities ([Shanghai](27643.md), Beijing, [Guangzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangzhou) and Shenzhen) are projected to be among the global top ten largest cities by [nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominal_GDP) according to a report by Oxford Economics.[^cite_note-94]

### Hong Kong and Macau

Main articles: [Economy of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Hong_Kong) and [Economy of Macau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Macau)

In accordance with the [one country, two systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_country,_two_systems) policy, the economies of the former British colony of Hong Kong and Portuguese colony of Macau formally preserve a capitalist system separate from mainland China.[^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96]

### Regional development

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  |  | [Eastern China](683693.md) |
|  |  | "[Rise of Central China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rise_of_Central_China_Plan)" |
|  |  | "[Revitalize Northeast China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Area_Revitalization_Plan)" |
|  |  | "[China Western Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Western_Development)" |

These strategies are aimed at the relatively underdeveloped regions of China in an attempt to address unequal development:

- [China Western Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Western_Development), designed to increase the economic situation of the western provinces through investment and development of natural resources.

- [Revitalize Northeast China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revitalize_Northeast_China), to rejuvenate the industrial bases in [Northeast China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_China). It covers the three provinces of [Heilongjiang](173816.md), [Jilin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jilin), and [Liaoning](155516.md), as well as the five eastern [prefectures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prefecture_\(China\)) of [Inner Mongolia](55064.md).

- [Rise of Central China Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rise_of_Central_China_Plan), to accelerate the development of its central regions. It covers six provinces: [Shanxi](81262.md), [Henan](166408.md), [Anhui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anhui), [Hubei](83319.md), [Hunan](166409.md), and [Jiangxi](173817.md).

- [Third Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Front_\(China\)) of the Cold War period, which focused on the southwestern provinces.

## Government

See also: [Five-year plans of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five-year_plans_of_China)



China and other major developing economies by [GDP per capita at purchasing-power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)_per_capita) (1990–2024) as the rapid economic growth of China is readily apparent

[^cite_note-97]

China engages in state-led investment and industrial policy with a significant state-owned enterprise sector.[^cite_note-98]<sup>: 11 </sup> The [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) (CCP) describes China's economic system as the [socialist market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_market_economy). To guide economic development, the Chinese central government adopts [five-year plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five-year_plans_of_China) that detail its economic priorities and essential policies. The [fifteenth five-year plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15th_Five-Year_Plan) (2026–2030) is currently being implemented, following the [fourteenth five-year plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_Five-Year_Plan) (2021-2025) which placed an emphasis on consumption-driven growth and technological self-sufficiency while China transitions from being an upper middle-income economy to a high-income economy.[^cite_note-99]

The [public sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_sector) plays a central role in China's economy.[^cite_note-100] In *Rural China Takes Off* (1999), Jean Oi identified reform-era China as a local variety of [developmental states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developmental_state), with "local governments in the lead role of the development process."[^cite_note-101] Yuen Yuen Ang's *How China Escaped the Poverty Trap* (2016) characterized the role of the state in Chinese development as "directed improvisation," a mixture of top-down direction by the central government and bottom-up improvisation among local governments,[^cite_note-\:2-102] producing a variety of subnational coevolutionary paths and distinct regional models,[^cite_note-\:2-102] rather than a single, fixed China model.[^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104]

Currently, China's development goals are to achieve the [Two Centenaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Centenaries), namely the material goal of China becoming a [moderately prosperous society in all respects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moderately_prosperous_society) by 2021 and the modernization goal of China becoming a "strong, democratic, civilized, harmonious and modern socialist country" by 2049, the 100th anniversary of the founding of the People's Republic.[^cite_note-105] China retains state control over the [commanding heights of the economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commanding_heights_of_the_economy) in key industries like infrastructure, telecommunications, and finance despite significant marketization of the economy since [reform and opening up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up).[^cite_note-Heilmann_2018-106]<sup>: 20 </sup> Specific mechanisms implementing government control of the commanding heights of the economy include public property rights, pervasive administrative involvement, and CCP supervision of senior managers.[^cite_note-Heilmann_2018-106]<sup>: 20 </sup>

The state is more likely to intervene in areas where the prices of goods and services are socially and politically sensitive.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 105 </sup> For example, China's government intervenes more actively in the commercial banking sector than in private equity, where significantly fewer households participate.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 100 </sup> The state's involvement in the allocation of finance, contracts, and resources facilitates Chinese government efforts to minimize the effects of market volatility.[^cite_note-Garlick_2024-108]<sup>: 3 </sup>

Local governments' level of authority and autonomy in economic decision-making is high, and they have played a major role in national economic development.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 1 </sup> Local governments may also offer incentives or subsidies to attract business.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 41 </sup> One common tax incentive is "three tax-free and three half-tax", through which businesses can be allowed not to pay corporate income tax for their first three years of operation, and pay a half rate for the next three years.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 41 </sup>

### State-owned enterprises

Main article: [State-owned enterprises of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-owned_enterprises_of_China)

China's SOEs perform functions that benefit the state.[^cite_note-Pieke_2022-110] Academic Wendy Leutert writes, "They contribute to central and local governments revenues through dividends and taxes, support urban employment, keep key input prices low, channel capital towards targeted industries and technologies, support sub-national redistribution to poorer interior and western provinces, and aid the state's response to natural disasters, financial crises and social instability."[^cite_note-Pieke_2022-110] SOE top management personnel is controlled by the [CCP's Organization Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_Department_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party).[^cite_note-111]<sup>: 140 </sup>

Almost 867,000 enterprises in China have a degree of state ownership, according to [Franklin Allen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_Allen) of [Imperial College London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_College_London).[^cite_note-XJ-112] As of 2017, China has more SOEs than any other country, and the most SOEs among large national companies.[^cite_note-Pieke_2022-110] State-owned enterprises accounted for over 60% of China's market capitalization in 2019[^cite_note-113] and generated 40% of China's GDP of US\$15.98 trillion dollars (101.36 trillion yuan) in 2020, with domestic and foreign private businesses and investment accounting for the remaining 60%.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115] As of the end of 2019, the total assets of all China's SOEs, including those operating in the financial sector, reached US\$58.97 trillion In 2015.[^cite_note-116] Ninety-one (91) of these SOEs belong to the 2020 Fortune Global 500 companies.[^cite_note-117] As of 2025, private firms represent 40% of the country's top-100 listed firms, mixed-ownership firms represent 15%, while state-owned enterprises represent 45%, according to the [Peterson Institute for International Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peterson_Institute_for_International_Economics).[^cite_note-118]

### Disputes over economic data

There exist disputes over reliability of official economic data. Foreign and some Chinese sources have claimed that official Chinese government statistics overstate China's economic growth.[^cite_note-Tang_2017-119][^cite_note-CM-120][^cite_note-121] However, several Western academics and institutions have stated that China's economic growth is higher than indicated by official figures.[^cite_note-Fernald_2013-122] Others, such as the [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md), state that while there's evidence China's GDP data is "smoothed", they believe that China's nominal and real GDP data are broadly accurate.[^cite_note-123] Still, others state that reported GDP growth is irreconcilable with data coming out of China.[^cite_note-124] According to 2007 documents obtained by WikiLeaks, [Liaoning](155516.md) Party Secretary and future Premier [Li Keqiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Keqiang) said he is far from confident in the country's GDP estimates, calling them "man-made" and unreliable and that data releases, especially the GDP numbers, should be used "for reference only".[^cite_note-125] In its place, he developed the [Li Keqiang index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Keqiang_index) as an alternative measurement of Chinese economic performance that uses three variables he preferred.[^cite_note-126] After meeting Alibaba's Jack Ma[^cite_note-127] in February in March 2025, China announced a 5 percent growth target at its annual sessions, despite ongoing trade tensions with the United States. *The Washington Post* reports that China's two annual political meetings, hosted by the National People's Congress and the Chinese People's Political Consultative Conference, set a growth target of "around 5 percent" for 2025 despite rising trade tensions with the United States, signaling Beijing's certainty in its recovery. Leaders underlined boosting consumption, supporting private-sector innovation, and growing fiscal tools such as a greater deficit ratio and special bonds to stabilize growth. Officials pledged to support exporters, draw foreign investment, and promote technological self-reliance, while encouraging private entrepreneurship. The sessions presented China as a global stabilizer and warned of countermeasures against trade barriers, showing a pragmatic, growth-oriented approach throughout geopolitical challenges.[^cite_note-128]

Chinese provinces and cities have long been suspected of cooking their numbers, with the focus on local government officials, whose performance is often assessed based on how well their respective economies have performed.[^cite_note-129] Local governments have come under increased scrutiny over economic data, with CCP general secretary [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) stating that economic data forgery "not only hurt our judgment of the economic situation, but also seriously undermined the Communist Party's ideas and truth-seeking style".[^cite_note-Tang_2017-119][^cite_note-130]

Satellite measurements of [light pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_pollution) are used by some analysts to model Chinese economic growth and suggest growth rate numbers in Chinese official data are more reliable, though are likely to be smoothed.[^cite_note-131] According to an article by the [Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Bank_of_St._Louis), China's official statistics are of a high quality compared to other developing, middle-income and low-income countries. In 2016, China was at the 83rd percentile of middle and low-income countries, up from the 38th percentile in 2004.[^cite_note-132] A study by the [Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Bank_of_San_Francisco) found that China's official GDP statistics are "significantly and positively correlated" with externally verifiable measures of economic activity such as import and export data from China's trade partners, suggesting that China's economic growth was no slower than the official figures indicated.[^cite_note-Fernald_2013-122]

Several Western academics and institutions have supported the claim that China's economy is likely to be underestimated.[^cite_note-Clark_2020-133][^cite_note-134][^cite_note-Arvind_2011-135][^cite_note-Fernald_2013-122][^cite_note-Rosen_2015-136][^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138] A paper by the US-based [National Bureau of Economic Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bureau_of_Economic_Research) claimed that China's economic growth may be higher than what is reported by official statistics.[^cite_note-139] An article by Hunter Clarka, Maxim Pinkovskiya and [Xavier Sala-i-Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xavier_Sala-i-Martin) published by [Science Direct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ScienceDirect) from [Elsevier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elsevier) in 2018 employs an innovative method of satellite-recorded nighttime lights, which the authors claim to be a best-unbiased predictor of the economic growth in Chinese cities. The results suggest that the Chinese economic growth rate is higher than the official reported data.[^cite_note-Clark_2020-133]

The study by [Daniel H. Rosen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_H._Rosen) and Beibei Bao, published by the [Center for Strategic and International Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Strategic_and_International_Studies) in 2015, showed that GDP in 2008 was actually 13–16 percent bigger than the official data, while 2013 GDP was accurately at \$10.5 trillion rather than the official figure at \$9.5 trillion.[^cite_note-Rosen_2015-136] According to a research conducted by [Arvind Subramanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arvind_Subramanian), a former economist at the [International Money Fund (IMF)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Money_Fund) and a senior fellow at the Peterson Institute for International Economics, the size of the Chinese economy by [Purchasing Power Parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) in 2010 was about \$14.8 trillion rather than an official estimate at \$10.1 trillion by IMF, meaning that China's GDP was underestimated by 47 percent.[^cite_note-Arvind_2011-135]

According to a 2019 research paper published by the [Brookings Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookings_Institution) adjusting the historical GDP time series using [value-added tax](52177473.md) data, which the authors said are "highly resistant to fraud and tampering",[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-Wildau_2019-141] China's economic growth may have been overstated by 1.7 percent each year between 2008 and 2016, meaning that the government may have been overstating the size of the Chinese economy by 12–16 percent in 2016.[^cite_note-Wildau_2019-141][^cite_note-142]

The Rhodium Group, an independent research [think tank](37101.md), has consistently questioned the accuracy of China's official GDP figures, estimating actual GDP growth in 2024 to be between 2.4% and 2.8%, significantly lower than the official claim of 4.8%.[^cite_note-143] As observers raised their skepticism of the Chinese economy, in 2025, the United States [Federal Reserve](10819.md) conducted economic research on whether China was growing at its 5% growth target. They concluded that Chinese official figures were not overstated, with alternative indicators, including trade, consumption, and the property sector data, closely aligned with the official GDP figure. China's strong supply-side economy for international trade and self-reliance offsets the weakness in consumption after the pandemic.[^cite_note-144]

China has shifted its approach to managing the private sector, moving from unpredictable crackdowns to a more stable, regulated system. In 2025, Xi Jinping signaled a new direction by meeting with Alibaba's Jack Ma, emphasizing the role of private firms in China's technological growth.[^cite_note-145] The meeting with Alibaba's Jack Ma and other private sector leaders signaled a warmer stance towards China's technology firms after many years of regulatory pressure. It also indicated that there was a warmer stance toward private tech and AI firms after Alibaba and Ant Group were previously under scrutiny. It now seemed that analysts saw Beijing as wanting to promote domestic private tech firms and broader AI development to compete with the rest of the world. The meeting, which came just weeks before the unveiling of China's DeepSeek's latest AI model, which provided low-cost alternatives to the US alternatives.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147]**&#x20;**&#x4C;egal protections for entrepreneurs have been codified, while the Chinese Communist Party has increased its presence in companies through party cells and "golden shares." This new model seeks to balance innovation with state control, particularly in high-tech sectors like AI and semiconductors.<https://www.foreignaffairs.com/china/chinas-private-sector-pivot>[^cite_note-148]

### National debt

Main article: [National debt of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_debt_of_China)

In 2022, China's total government debt was approximately CN¥ 94 trillion (US\$ 14 trillion), or 77.1% of [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP).[^cite_note-IMFWEO-149] In 2014, many analysts expressed concern over the overall size of China's government debt.[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] At the end of 2014, the International Monetary Fund reported that China's general government gross debt-to-GDP ratio was 41.44 percent.[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155] In 2015, a report by the International Monetary Fund concluded that China's public debt is relatively low "and on a stable path in all standard stress tests except for the scenario with contingent liability shocks", such as "a large-scale bank recapitalization or financial system bailout to deal, for example, with a potential rise in [NPLs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-performing_loan) from deleveraging".[^cite_note-156] Per the [State Administration of Foreign Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Administration_of_Foreign_Exchange)'s 2019 [balance of payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balance_of_payments) report, the external debt balance of the entire Chinese government at year end was 2% of GDP.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 104 </sup>

Chinese authorities have dismissed analysts' worries, insisting "the country still has room to increase government debt".[^cite_note-157] Former Fed Chairman [Ben Bernanke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Bernanke), earlier in 2016, commented that "the ... debt pile facing China \[is] an 'internal' problem", given the majority of the borrowings was issued in local currency.[^cite_note-158] A 2019 survey by the [OECD](33853117.md) found that China's corporate debt is higher than other major countries.[^cite_note-159] [Shadow banking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_banking) has risen in China, posing risks to the financial system.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] Off-the-books debt is a grey area, but estimates place the amount for local governments alone as high as \$9 trillion[^cite_note-162] or 63 trillion yuan, up from estimates of around 30 trillion yuan in 2020.[^cite_note-163]

### Taxation, regulatory environment and government revenues

Main article: [Taxation in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_China)

Though China's economy has expanded rapidly, its regulatory environment has not kept pace. Since Deng Xiaoping's open market reforms, the growth of new businesses has outpaced the government's ability to regulate them. This has created a situation where businesses, faced with mounting competition and poor oversight, take drastic measures to increase [profit margins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profit_margin), often at the expense of [consumer safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_safety). This issue became more prominent in 2007, with a number of restrictions being placed on problematic Chinese exports by the United States.[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165]

#### Anti-monopoly and competition law

The Chinese technology sector has been characterized as being dominated by few, larger entities including [Ant Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant_Group) and Tencent.[^cite_note-166] There have been attempts by the [Xi Jinping Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Administration) to enforce [economic competition rules](666256.md), and probes into Alibaba and Tencent have been launched by Chinese economic regulators.[^cite_note-167]

The crackdown on tech giants and internet companies during the [2020–2021 Xi Jinping Administration reform spree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932021_Xi_Jinping_Administration_reform_spree) was followed with calls by the [Politburo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politburo_Standing_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) against monopolistic practices by commercial retail giants like [Alibaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alibaba_Group).[^cite_note-168] In August 2024 China's market regulator ended its antitrust review of Alibaba Group Holding after fining them in 2021. The company was fined for US\$2.8 billion in 2021. The three years of regulatory scrutiny compelled Alibaba to comply with the antitrust authority.[^cite_note-169] Studies showed that the cumulative effects of the investigation on Alibaba resulted in 17% to 25% decline in abnormal stock returns.[^cite_note-170] In March 2021, Xi stated that China would strengthen its [antitrust](666256.md) enforcement in an effort to advance what he described as the healthy and sustainable development of the platform economy.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 100 </sup> Two major institutional changes resulted.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 100 </sup> In November 2021, China upgraded the bureaucratic status of the [State Administration for Market Regulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Administration_for_Market_Regulation)'s antitrust bureau.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 100–101 </sup> In June 2022, it made major amendments to the [Anti-Monopoly Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti_Monopoly_Law_of_China), including explicit language, focused on regulation of the platform economy.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 101 </sup>

[Corruption](3608404.md) has been a significant problem in the [People's Republic of China](5405.md). Like other socialist economies that have undertaken economic reforms, such as [post-Soviet Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) and [Central Asia](6742.md), [reform-era China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_China_\(1976%E2%80%931989\)) has experienced increasing levels of corruption.[^cite_note-Corruption_in_China_yan2-172] In [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International)'s 2025 [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index), China ranked 76th out of 182 countries, with a score of 43 out of 100.[^cite_note-Corruption_in_China_CPI2025-China-173]

Public surveys on the mainland since the late 1980s have shown that corruption is among the top concerns of the general public. According to Yan Sun, Associate Professor of Political Science at the [City University of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_University_of_New_York), it was cadre corruption, rather than a demand for democracy as such, that lay at the root of the social dissatisfaction that led to the [1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre](44534.md).[^cite_note-Corruption_in_China_yan2-172] Corruption undermines the legitimacy of the authoritarian [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) (CCP) government, adds to [economic inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality), undermines the environment, and fuels social unrest.[^cite_note-Corruption_in_China_pei2007-174]

Since the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests and massacre, corruption has not slowed as a result of greater [economic freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_freedom), but instead has grown more entrenched and severe in its character and scope. In popular perception, there are more dishonest CCP officials than honest ones, a reversal of the views held in the first decade of the [reform and opening up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up) period of the 1980s.[^cite_note-Corruption_in_China_yan2-172] Chinese political scientist [Minxin Pei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minxin_Pei) argued in 2007 that failure to contain widespread corruption is among the most serious threats to China's future economic and political stability.[^cite_note-Corruption_in_China_pei2007-174] He estimated that year that bribery, kickbacks, theft, and the waste of public funds cost at least three percent of GDP.

By the 2000s and early 2010s, corruption had grown to be an extensive problem, impacting government administration, [armed forces](66890.md), [law enforcement](265856.md),[^cite_note-175] [healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_China)[^cite_note-176] and [education](407843.md).[^cite_note-177] [CCP cadre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadre_system_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) corruption in China has been subject to significant media attention since [CCP General Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) announced his [anti-corruption campaign](45655698.md) following the CCP's [18th National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/18th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), which was held in November 2012.[^cite_note-178] [Many high-ranking government and military officials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Officials_implicated_by_the_anti-corruption_campaign_in_China_\(2012%E2%80%932017\)) have been found guilty of corruption because of this campaign. According to analysts Neil Thomas and Wang Shengyu, the campaign also led to an overall decrease in petty bribery and open embezzlement. According to the [World Bank](36752.md)’s [Worldwide Governance Indicators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worldwide_Governance_Indicators), China has advanced under Xi on metrics of "control of corruption" and "government effectiveness", moving from around the same as other upper-middle-income countries to well above the average.[^cite_note-Corruption_in_China_\:62-179] Other academics have found that recent anti-corruption campaigns have increased the value of personal connections and driven a rise in other forms of corruption.[^cite_note-Corruption_in_China_\:5-180][^cite_note-Corruption_in_China_\:3-181] Writing in 2024, academic Lan Xiaohuan states that China exhibits relatively little petty corruption compared with other [developing countries](78449.md).[^cite_note-Corruption_in_China_\:6-182]<sup>: 115 </sup>

### Inflation



Chinese inflation 1987–2022

As of 2025, China had experienced three inflation peaks: 1987–1989 (18.8%), 1993–1996 (peaking at 24%), and 2008 (8.2%).[^cite_note-\:2025MesaLago-184]<sup>: 47 </sup>

According to China's National Bureau of Statistics (NBS), the producer price index rose by 2.8 percent in April 2026 compared with the same month a year earlier. The increase was attributed to rising global energy and raw material prices driven by the war in the Middle East.[^cite_note-185]

### Investment cycles



[Beijing Financial Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Financial_Street)

Chinese investment has always been highly cyclical.[^cite_note-186]

In China, the majority of investment is carried out by entities that are at least partially state-owned. Most of these are under the control of local governments. Thus, booms are primarily the result of [perverse incentives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perverse_incentives) at the local-government level.[^cite_note-187] Unlike entrepreneurs in a free-enterprise economy, Chinese local officials are motivated primarily by political considerations. As their performance evaluations are based, to a large extent, on GDP growth within their jurisdictions, they have a strong incentive to promote large-scale investment projects.[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]

A typical cycle begins with a relaxation of central government credit and industrial policy. This allows local governments to push investment aggressively, both through state-sector entities they control directly and by offering investment-promotion incentives to private investors and enterprises outside their jurisdictions.[^cite_note-190] The resulting boom puts upward pressure on prices and may also result in shortages of key inputs such as coal and electricity (as was the case in 2003).[^cite_note-191] Once inflation has risen to a level at which it begins to threaten social stability, the central government will intervene by tightening enforcement of industrial and credit policy. Projects that went ahead without required approvals will be halted. Bank lending to particular types of investors will be restricted. Credit then becomes tight and investment growth begins to decline.[^cite_note-192]

### Unemployment

Further information: [Xiagang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiagang)

Since the Reform and Open Up, unemployment has been a recurring issue that the Chinese economy grapples with. In 1979–80 there were twenty million unemployed people.[^cite_note-193] The reforms in China's state-owned enterprises had led to the dismantling of life-long employment system colloquially coined as [iron rice bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_rice_bowl) (铁饭碗), a process that later culminated in large-scale layoffs of urban workers during the period known as [Xiagang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiagang) (*下岗*), which contributed to drastic rise in urban unemployment since the 1990s. The situation of mass unemployment was later improved following [China's admission by the World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_and_the_World_Trade_Organization) in 2001.

Recently, China's unemployment landscape has become a pressing concern, particularly among its youth. Official figures indicate a relatively stable urban surveyed unemployment rate of 5.0% as of late 2024, with annual unemployment projected to decline slightly from 5.2% in 2023 to 5.1% in 2024. However, youth unemployment paints a more troubling picture. Officially, the unemployment rate for individuals aged 16 to 24 peaked at 21.3% in mid-2023, before declining to 16.1% by November 2024. Yet, independent estimates suggest that the true youth unemployment rate may have been significantly higher, with some analysts, including a prominent Chinese professor, suggesting it could have reached as high as 46.5%, while others speculated it may have approached or exceeded 50%. Reports have also surfaced about the creation of "fake jobs" or positions that exist on paper but provide no meaningful work or income, aimed at artificially lowering the reported unemployment figures. These issues, compounded by a slowing economy, a prolonged property market crisis, and insufficient demand for skilled labor, have led many young Chinese to "lie flat" or retreat from the workforce altogether. As China implements reforms to improve labor conditions and stimulate economic growth, achieving a projected 5% GDP growth target, youth unemployment and underemployment remain critical challenges for policymakers and society alike.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195]

## Financial and banking system

Main articles: [Banking in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_in_China), [List of banks in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_banks_in_China), [Foreign-exchange reserves of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign-exchange_reserves_of_China), [Hedge fund industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hedge_fund_industry_in_China), [China Venture Capital Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Venture_Capital_Association), [List of venture capital firms § Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_venture_capital_firms#Asia), [Securities industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Securities_industry_in_China), and [Insurance industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurance_industry_in_China)



China bond yields&#x20;

&#x20; 30 year bond

&#x20; 10 year bond

&#x20; 2 year bond

&#x20; 1 year bond

China has the world's largest total banking sector assets of around \$45.838 trillion (309.41 trillion CNY) with \$42.063 trillion in total deposits and other liabilities.[^cite_note-196] Most of China's financial institutions are state-owned and governed.[^cite_note-197] The chief instruments of [financial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finance) and [fiscal](132645.md) control are the [People's Bank of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Bank_of_China) (PBC) and the [Ministry of Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Finance_\(China\)), both under the authority of the [State Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Council_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China). The People's Bank of China replaced the [Central Bank of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_China) in 1950 and gradually took over [private banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_bank). It fulfills many of the functions of other [central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_bank) and [commercial banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_bank). It issues the currency, controls circulation, and plays an important role in disbursing budgetary expenditures. Additionally, it administers the accounts, payments, and receipts of government organizations and other bodies, which enables it to exert thorough supervision over their financial and general performances in consideration of the government's economic plans. The PBC is also responsible for [international trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_trade) and other overseas [transactions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_transaction). [Remittances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remittance) by [overseas Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Chinese) are managed by the [Bank of China](303789.md) (BOC), which has a number of branch offices in several countries.

Other financial institutions that are crucial, include the [China Development Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Development_Bank) (CDB), which funds economic development and directs [foreign investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment); the [Agricultural Bank of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_Bank_of_China) (ABC), which provides for the agricultural sector; the [China Construction Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Construction_Bank) (CCB), which is responsible for capitalizing a portion of overall investment and for providing [capital funds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_fund) for certain industrial and construction enterprises; and the [Industrial and Commercial Bank of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_and_Commercial_Bank_of_China) (ICBC), which conducts ordinary commercial transactions and acts as a [savings bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savings_bank) for the public. China initiated the founding of the [Asian Infrastructure Investment Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Infrastructure_Investment_Bank) in 2015 and the [Silk Road Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road_Fund) in 2014, an investment fund of the Chinese government to foster increased investment and provide financial supports in countries along the [One Belt, One Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Belt,_One_Road).[^cite_note-198]



[Shanghai Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Stock_Exchange) (SSE)

China's economic reforms greatly increased the economic role of the [banking system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_in_China). In theory any enterprises or individuals can go to the banks to obtain loans outside the state plan, in practice, 75% of state bank loans go to [State Owned Enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government-owned_corporation). (SOEs)[^cite_note-199] Even though nearly all investment [capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_\(economics\)) was previously provided on a [grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grant_\(money\)) basis according to the state plan, policy has since the start of the reform shifted to a loan basis through the various state-directed financial institutions. It is estimated that, as of 2011, 14 trillion Yuan in loans was outstanding to local governments. Much of that total is believed by outside observers to be nonperforming.[^cite_note-200] Increasing amounts of funds are made available through the banks for economic and commercial purposes. Foreign sources of capital have also increased. China has received loans from the [World Bank](36752.md) and several [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_and_the_United_Nations) programs, as well as from countries (particularly [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Japanese_relations)) and, to a lesser extent, commercial banks. [Hong Kong](13404.md) has been a major conduit of this investment, as well as a source itself. On 23 February 2012, the PBC evinced its inclination to liberalize its capital markets when it circulated a telling ten-year timetable.[^cite_note-201] Following on the heels of this development, Shenzhen banks were able to launch cross-border yuan remittances for individuals, a significant shift in the PBC's [capital control](5015944.md) strictures since Chinese nationals had been previously barred from transferring their yuan to overseas account.[^cite_note-202]

China has four of the world's top ten most competitive [financial centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Financial_Centres_Index) (Shanghai, Hong Kong, Beijing, and Shenzhen), more than any other country.[^cite_note-Long_Finance_2020-203] China has three of the world's ten [largest stock exchanges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_stock_exchanges) (Shanghai, [Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Stock_Exchange) and [Shenzhen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shenzhen_Stock_Exchange)), both by [market capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_capitalization) and by [trade volume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_volume).[^cite_note-ValueWalk_2019-46][^cite_note-204] As of 12 October 2020, the total market capitalization of mainland Chinese stock markets, consisting of the Shanghai Stock Exchange and Shenzhen Stock Exchange, topped US\$10 trillion, excluding the Hong Kong Stock Exchange, with about US\$5.9 trillion.[^cite_note-205]

As of June 2020, foreign investors had bought a total of US\$440 billion in Chinese stocks, representing about 2.9% of the total value, and indicating that foreign investors scooped up a total of US\$156.6 billion in the stocks just in the first half of 2020.[^cite_note-206] The total value of China's bond market topped US\$15.4 trillion, ranked above that of Japan and the UK, and second only to that of the U.S. with US\$40 trillion, as of the beginning of September 2020.[^cite_note-207] As of September 2020, foreign holdings of Chinese bonds reached US\$388 billion, or 2.5%, of the total value, notwithstanding an increase by 44.66% year on year.[^cite_note-208]

### Stock markets



Shanghai Composite Index 1991–2022

As of 2024, China has the second-largest [equity markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equity_markets) and [futures markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Futures_markets) in the world, as well as the third largest [bond market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bond_market).[^cite_note-Curtis\&Klaus-209]<sup>: 153 </sup> China's stock market exchanges include the [Beijing Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Stock_Exchange), the [Shanghai Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Stock_Exchange) (including the [STAR Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Stock_Exchange_STAR_Market)), the [Shenzhen Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shenzhen_Stock_Exchange), and the [Hong Kong Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Stock_Exchange). China's stock market is relatively underdeveloped in comparison to other aspects of its economy.[^cite_note-Jin_2023-210]<sup>: 148–150 </sup>

To be listed on China's stock exchange, companies must demonstrate good financial standing (including sustained profitability), solid corporate governance, including a board of independent directors, supervisory board, auditing, and no history of misreporting or fraud, and have a market capitalization equivalent to at least US\$4 million.[^cite_note-Marquis_2022-87]<sup>: 271–272 </sup> The government regulates [initial public offerings](63879.md), encouraging them when the market is high in an effort to cool down prices and prohibiting them when the market is low.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 109 </sup>

When the stock markets re-opened in the PRC period in 1990, most of the listed companies were state-owned enterprises; this was part of an experiment in subjecting SOEs to market discipline.[^cite_note-Jin_2023-210]<sup>: 152–153 </sup> The Shanghai and Shenzhen stock exchanges were under municipal control and termed "experimental points" until 1997.[^cite_note-Heilmann_2018-106]<sup>: 102–103 </sup> In 1997, the central government brought the exchanges under central government control and affirmed that the exchanges had a legitimate role in the socialist market economy.[^cite_note-Heilmann_2018-106]<sup>: 102 </sup> In 2015, a [stock market plunge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015%E2%80%932016_Chinese_stock_market_turbulence) in China eliminated \$2 trillion of global stock market value.[^cite_note-Jin_2023-210]<sup>: 147 </sup>

### Currency system

See also: [Renminbi](66958.md)



[USD](18717338.md)/[CNY](66958.md) exchange rate 1981–2022

The renminbi ("people's currency") is the currency of China, denominated as the [yuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuan_\(currency\)), subdivided into 10 [jiao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_yuan) or 100 [fen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_yuan). The renminbi is issued by the People's Bank of China, the monetary authority of China. The [ISO 4217](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_4217) abbreviation is CNY, although also commonly abbreviated as "RMB". As of 2005, the yuan was generally considered by outside observers to be undervalued by about 30–40%.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212] In 2011, the IMF stated that the renminbi was undervalued by 23%.[^cite_note-\:1-213] In 2026, the IMF stated that the renminbi was undervalued by 16%.[^cite_note-\:1-213]

There is a complex relationship between China's [balance of trade](4810.md), inflation, measured by the [consumer price index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_price_index) and the value of its currency. Despite allowing the value of the yuan to "float", China's central bank has decisive ability to control its value with relationship to other currencies[^cite_note-214] An article published in International Review of Economics & Finance in 2010 by Mete Feridun (University of Greenwich Business School) and his colleagues provide empirical evidence that financial development fosters economic growth in China.[^cite_note-215]

The renminbi is held in a floating [exchange-rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exchange-rate) system managed primarily against the [US dollar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_dollar). China has moved to an exchange rate system that references a basket of currencies and has allowed the renminbi to fluctuate at a daily rate of up to half a percent. A market-based "representative" exchange rate against the U.S. dollar is one of the requirements for designation of a currency as one with [Special Drawing Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_drawing_rights) (SDR) by the International Monetary Fund (IMF), one of China's goals.[^cite_note-216] Since the late-2000s, China has sought to [internationalize the renminbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internationalization_of_the_renminbi).[^cite_note-217] As of 2013, the RMB is the 8th most widely traded currency in the world.[^cite_note-218] The internationalization of the Chinese economy continues to affect the standardized economic forecast officially launched in China by the [Purchasing Managers Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_Managers_Index) in 2005.

### Nonfinancial debt

China aims for roughly 5% GDP growth and 2% inflation, using higher fiscal deficits and expanded local government bond issuance to replace shadow LGFVs with official debt. Total government borrowing, including LGFVs, has risen sharply to 124% of GDP, with local government debt over 60% and overall non-financial debt at 312%. In 2014, nonfinancial debt to GDP stood at \~140%. Despite accommodative monetary policies, weak domestic consumption and uncertain exports make growth targets challenging.[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] Nonfinancial debt is the total nominal value of outstanding debt instruments issued by the non-financial corporate sector. This includes corporate, household and government debt.[^cite_note-221]

Household debt has risen past 60% of GDP, mainly from mortgages and leveraged investments, affecting 5–7% of adults. High savings provide some buffer, but a property slump, youth unemployment, and aggressive debt collection heighten financial and social stress. Limited regulations, including cautious personal bankruptcy laws, leave many households exposed while the government balances support with broader economic stability.[^cite_note-222]

## Sectors

According to [Fortune Global](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_Global_500), of the world's 500 largest companies, 135 are headquartered in China.[^cite_note-Global_500-44] As of 2023, mainland China and Hong Kong were home to 324 largest listed companies measured by [revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revenue) in the [Fortune Global 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes_Global_2000), ranking second globally.[^cite_note-223] China is also home to more than two hundred privately held technology startups (tech unicorns), each with a valuation of over \$1 billion, the highest number in the world.[^cite_note-224]

### Agriculture, aquaculture, livestock, fishing and forestry

Main pages: [Agriculture in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_China), [Aquaculture in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquaculture_in_China), [Fishing industry in China](20811073.md), and [Category\:Forestry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Forestry_in_China)

See also: [Food policy in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_policy_in_China), [Wine in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wine_in_China), [Silk industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_industry_in_China), [Beer in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_in_China), and [Wet markets in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_markets_in_China)

[Peanut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peanut) harvest in [Jiangxia District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiangxia_District), [Hubei](83319.md)

China is the world's largest producer and consumer of agricultural products – and some 300 million Chinese [farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farm) workers are in the industry, mostly laboring on pieces of land about the size of U.S. farms. Virtually all [arable land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arable_land) is used for food [crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crop). China is the world's largest producer of [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice) and is among the principal sources of [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat), corn ([maize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize)), [tobacco](30942.md), [soybeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybeans), [potatoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potato), [sorghum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorghum), [peanuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peanut), [tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea), [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet), [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley), [oilseed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oilseed), [pork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pork), and [fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish). Major non-food crops, including cotton, other fibers, and [oilseeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oilseed), furnish China with a small proportion of its foreign trade revenue. Agricultural exports, such as vegetables and fruits, fish and shellfish, grain and meat products, are exported to Hong Kong. Yields are high because of [intensive cultivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intensive_cultivation), for example, China's cropland area is only 75% of the U.S. total, but China still produces about 30% more crops and livestock than the United States. China hopes to further increase agricultural production through improved plant stocks, [fertilizers](37401.md), and technology.



Production of [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat) from 1961 to 2004 (data from [FAO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FAO) in 2005, y-axis: production in metric tons)

According to the United Nations [World Food Programme](33582.md), in 2022, China fed eighteen percent of the world's population with only seven percent of the world's arable land.[^cite_note-225]

[Animal husbandry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_husbandry) constitutes the second most important component of agricultural production. China is the world's leading producer of pigs, chickens, and eggs, and it also has sizable herds of sheep and cattle. Since the mid-1970s, greater emphasis has been placed on increasing the [livestock](25160767.md) output. China has a long tradition of ocean and freshwater fishing and of [aquaculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquaculture). [Pond raising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pond_raising) has always been important and has been increasingly emphasized to supplement coastal and inland [fisheries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fisheries) threatened by [overfishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overfishing) and to provide such valuable export commodities as [prawns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prawn). China is also unmatched in the size and reach of its fishing armada with anywhere from 200,000 to 800,000 boats, some as far afield as Argentina. Fueled primarily by government subsidies, its growth and activities have largely gone unchecked.[^cite_note-226]



Timber transported from a woodlot in the hills of [Zhangpu County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhangpu_County), Fujian

Environmental problems such as floods, drought, and erosion pose serious threats to farming in many parts of the country. The wholesale destruction of forests gave way to an energetic [reforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reforestation) program that proved inadequate, and forest resources are still fairly meagre.[^cite_note-227] The principal forests are found in the [Qin Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qinling) and the central mountains and on the [Yunnan–Guizhou Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yunnan%E2%80%93Guizhou_Plateau). Because they are inaccessible, the Qinling forests are not worked extensively, and much of the country's [timber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timber) comes from [Heilongjiang](173816.md), [Jilin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jilin), [Sichuan](65185.md), and [Yunnan](166410.md).

[Western China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_China), comprising [Tibet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibet_Autonomous_Region), [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang), and [Qinghai](173818.md), has little agricultural significance except for areas of [floriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floriculture) and [cattle raising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle_raising). Rice, China's most important crop, is dominant in the southern provinces and many of the farms here yield two [harvests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvests) a year. In the north, wheat is of the greatest importance, while in [central China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_China) wheat and rice vie with each other for the top place. [Millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet) and [kaoliang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaoliang) (a variety of grain sorghum) are grown mainly in the northeast and some central provinces, which, together with some northern areas, also provide considerable quantities of [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley). Most of the [soybean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybean) crop is derived from the north and the northeast; corn (maize) is grown in the center and the north, while [tea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea) comes mainly from the warm and humid hilly areas of the south. Cotton is grown extensively in the central provinces, but it is also found to a lesser extent in the southeast and in the north. [Tobacco](30942.md) comes from the center and parts of the south. Other important crops are potatoes, [sugar beets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_beet), and oilseeds.



Fish ponds near [Daye, Hubei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daye,_Hubei)

In the past decade, the government has been encouraging agricultural mechanization and [land consolidation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_consolidation) to raise yields and compensate for the loss of rural workers who have migrated to the cities.[^cite_note-228] According to statistics by the UN Food and Agriculture Organization, the annual growth rate of agricultural mechanization in China is 6.4%. By 2014, the integrated mechanization rate had risen to nearly 60%, with the rate for wheat surpassing 90% and that for maize approaching 80%.[^cite_note-229] In addition to standard agricultural equipment like tractors, China's agriculture cooperatives have begun using high-tech equipment, including unmanned aerial vehicles, which are used to spray crops with pesticides.[^cite_note-230] Good progress has been made in increasing [water conservancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_conservancy), and about half the cultivated land is under irrigation.

In the late 1970s and early 1980s, economic reforms were introduced. First of all this began with the shift of farming work to a [system of household responsibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Responsibility_system) and a phasing out of [collectivized agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_farming). Later this expanded to include a gradual liberalization of price controls; [fiscal](132645.md) [decentralization](49139.md); massive privatization of state enterprises, thereby allowing a wide variety of [private enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_enterprise) in the [services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_sector) and [light manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_manufacturing); the foundation of a diversified [banking system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_system) (but with large amounts of state control); the development of a [stock market](52328.md); and the opening of the economy to increased [foreign trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_trade) and foreign investment.

### Housing and construction

Main articles: [Real estate in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_estate_in_China) and [Housing in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_in_China)

In 2010, China became the world's largest market for construction.[^cite_note-Curtis\&Klaus-209]<sup>: 112 </sup> It has remained the world's largest through at least 2024.[^cite_note-Curtis\&Klaus-209]<sup>: 112 </sup> The real estate industry is about 20% of the Chinese economy.[^cite_note-231] As of 2023, real property accounts for 60% of Chinese household assets.[^cite_note-Jin_2023-210]<sup>: 161 </sup> Also as of 2023, China has the highest rate of home ownership in the world.[^cite_note-Jin_2023-210]<sup>: 170 </sup> 90% of urban households own their home.[^cite_note-Jin_2023-210]<sup>: 170 </sup>

Compared to other nations, investing in stock markets and other assets is harder due to currency controls within the country. As a result, many Chinese citizens own multiple properties, as they are one of the few ways in which it is comparatively easy to grow and preserve wealth. Due to this, many economists have speculated about a property bubble within the Chinese economy.[^cite_note-232] On 16 July 2020, *The Wall Street Journal* reported that the housing market within the Chinese economy had grown to US\$52 trillion, eclipsing the US 2008 housing market before the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis).[^cite_note-233]

### Energy and mineral resources

See also: [Energy policy of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_China) and [Electricity sector in China](20184277.md)
