Over the years, large subsidies were built into the price structure of certain commodities and these subsidies grew substantially in the late 1970s and 1980s.[^cite_note-237] Since 1980, China's energy production has grown dramatically, as has the proportion allocated to domestic consumption. Some 80 percent of all power is generated from fossil fuel at [thermal plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermal_plant), with about 17 percent at [hydroelectric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric) installations; only about two percent is from [nuclear energy](22153.md), mainly from plants located in [Guangdong](65109.md) and [Zhejiang](96602.md).[^cite_note-238] Though China has rich overall energy potential, most have yet to be developed. In addition, the geographical distribution of energy puts most of these resources relatively far from their major industrial users. The northeast is rich in [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal) and [oil](4207510.md), the central part of north China has abundant coal, and the southwest has immense [hydroelectric potential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric_potential). But the industrialized regions around [Guangzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangzhou) and the [Lower Yangtze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yangtze_River) region around Shanghai have too little energy, while there is relatively little [heavy industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_industry) located near major energy resource areas other than in the southern part of the northeast.

Due in large part to environmental concerns, China has wanted to shift China's current [energy mix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_mix) from a heavy reliance on coal, which accounts for 70–75% of China's energy, toward greater reliance on oil, [natural gas](22131.md), [renewable energy](13556279.md), and [nuclear power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_China). China has closed thousands of [coal mines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_mines) over the past five to ten years to cut [overproduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overproduction). Since 1993, China has been a net importer of oil, a large portion of which comes from the Middle East. According to Chinese statistics, this has reduced coal production by over 25%. As of 2023, solar power has become cheaper than coal-fired power in China.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 167 </sup>

By 2010, rapidly rising wages and a general increase in the standard of living had put increased energy use on a collision course with the need to reduce [carbon emissions](21350772.md) in order to control [global warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming).[^cite_note-World_Bank-90] There were diligent efforts to increase [energy efficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efficient_energy_use) and increase use of [renewable sources](25784.md); over 1,000 inefficient power plants had been closed, but projections continued to show a dramatic rise in carbon emissions from burning [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel).[^cite_note-239]



Historical annual [CO<sub>2</sub> emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_carbon_dioxide_emissions) for the top six countries and confederations

While not the largest source of historical cumulative emissions, today China accounts for one quarter of global greenhouse gas emissions.[^cite_note-240] On a per capita basis, China's emissions in 2019 (9 tonnes CO<sub>2</sub>-equivalent \[tCO<sub>2</sub>e] per year) surpass those of the European Union (7.6 tCO<sub>2</sub>e) but remain slightly below the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) average (10.7 tCO<sub>2</sub>e) and well below the United States average (17.6 tCO<sub>2</sub>e). However, the carbon intensity of China's GDP—the amount of carbon used to generate a unit of output—remains relatively high.[^cite_note-World_Bank-90] To avoid the long-term socioeconomic cost[^cite_note-241] of environmental [pollution in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pollution_in_China),[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] it has been suggested by [Nicholas Stern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Stern,_Baron_Stern_of_Brentford) and Fergus Green of the [Grantham Research Institute on Climate Change and the Environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grantham_Research_Institute_on_Climate_Change_and_the_Environment) that the economy of China be shifted to more advanced industrial development with low carbon dioxide emissions and better allocation of national resources to innovation and R\&D for sustainable economic growth in order to reduce the impact of China's heavy industry. This is in accord with the planning goals of the central government.[^cite_note-244] Contrary to the publicized goals, China is building a large number of coal fired power plants and it carbon emissions could further increase.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246]

#### Mining

Main article: [Mining industry of China](58230808.md)

See also: [List of mines in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mines_in_China)

This section is an excerpt from [Mining industry of China](58230808.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Mining_industry_of_China\&action=edit)]



A coal mine near [Hailar District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hailar_District)

As of 2022, more than 200 types of minerals are actively explored or mined in the [People's Republic of China](5405.md) (PRC). These resources are widely but not evenly distributed throughout the country. Taken as a whole, China's economy and exports do not rely on the mining industry, but the industry is critical to various subnational governments of the PRC.

Mining is extensively regulated in the PRC and involves numerous regulatory bodies. The [state](23239.md) owns all mineral rights, regardless of the ownership of the land on which the minerals are located. Mining rights can be obtained upon government approval, and payment of mining and prospecting fees.

During the [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong) era, mineral exploration and mining was limited to [state-owned enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-owned_enterprises_of_China) and collectively owned enterprises and private exploration of mineral resources was largely prohibited. The industry was opened to private enterprises during the [reform and opening up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up) in the 1980s and became increasingly marketized in the 1990s. In the mid-2000s, the Chinese government sought to consolidate the industry due to concerns about underutilization of resources, workplace safety, and environmental harm. During that period, state-owned enterprises purchased smaller privately owned mines. China's mining industry grew substantially and the period from the early 2000s to 2012 is often referred to as a "golden decade" in the mining industry.

#### Hydroelectric resources

Main article: [Hydroelectricity in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectricity_in_China)



Three Gorges Dam

China has an abundant potential for [hydroelectric power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric_power) production due to its considerable river network and mountainous terrain. Most of the total hydroelectric capacity is situated in the [southwest of the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_China), where coal supplies are poor but [demand for energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_demand) is rising swiftly. The potential in the northeast is fairly small, but it was there that the first hydroelectric stations were built – by the Japanese during its occupation of [Manchuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchuria).[^cite_note-247]

#### Coal

Main article: [Coal in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_in_China)



Coal mining in [Inner Mongolia](55064.md)

China is well-endowed with mineral resources,[^cite_note-248] the most important of which is [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal). China's mineral resources include large reserves of coal and [iron ore](145352.md), plus adequate to abundant supplies of nearly all other industrial [minerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minerals). Although coal deposits are widely scattered (some types of coal is found in every province), most of the total is located in the northern part of the country. The province of [Shanxi](81262.md), in fact, is thought to contain about half of the total; other important coal-bearing provinces include [Heilongjiang](173816.md), [Liaoning](155516.md), [Jilin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jilin), [Hebei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebei), and [Shandong](105032.md).[^cite_note-TED-249] Apart from these northern provinces, significant quantities of coal are present in [Sichuan](65185.md), and there are some deposits of importance in [Guangdong](65109.md), [Guangxi](47836950.md), [Yunnan](166410.md), and [Guizhou](173815.md).[^cite_note-TED-249] A large part of the country's reserves consists of good [bituminous coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bituminous_coal), but there are also large deposits of [lignite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lignite). [Anthracite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthracite) is present in several places (especially [Liaoning](155516.md), [Guizhou](173815.md), and [Henan](166408.md)), but overall, it is not very significant.[^cite_note-250]

To ensure a more even distribution of coal supplies and to reduce the strain on the less than adequate [transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation) network, the authorities pressed for the development of a large number of small, locally run [mines](208413.md) throughout the country. This campaign was energetically pursued after the 1960s, with the result that thousands of small pits have been established, and they produce more than half the country's coal. This output, however, is typically expensive and is used for local consumption. It has also led to a less than stringent implementation of safety measures in these unregulated mines, which cause several thousands of deaths each year.[^cite_note-251]

Coal makes up the bulk of China's energy consumption (55% in 2021), and China is the largest producer and consumer of coal in the world. As China's economy continues to grow, China's coal demand is projected to rise significantly. Although coal's share of China's overall energy consumption will decrease, coal consumption will continue to rise in absolute terms. China's continued and increasing reliance on coal as a power source has contributed significantly to putting China on the path to becoming the world's largest emitter of [acid rain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid_rain)-causing [sulfur dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfur_dioxide) and [greenhouse gases](21350772.md), including [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide).

#### Oil and natural gas

Main articles: [Petroleum industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_industry_in_China), [Oil shale in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_shale_in_China), and [Natural gas in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_in_China)



Chinese oil reserves

China's onshore [oil](4207510.md) resources are mostly located in the [Northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_China) and in [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang), [Gansu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gansu), [Qinghai](173818.md), [Sichuan](65185.md), [Shandong](105032.md), and [Henan](166408.md) provinces. [Oil shale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_shale) is found in a number of places, especially at [Fushun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fushun) in Liaoning, where the deposits overlie the coal reserves, as well as in Guangdong. High quality [light oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_crude_oil) has been found in the [Pearl River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearl_River_\(China\)) estuary of the [South China Sea](74209.md), the [Qaidam Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qaidam_Basin) in Qinghai, and the [Tarim Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarim_Basin) in Xinjiang. The country consumes most of its oil output but does export some crude oil and oil products. China has explored and developed oil deposits in the [South China Sea](74209.md) and [East China Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_China_Sea), the [Yellow Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_Sea), the [Gulf of Tonkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Tonkin), and the [Bohai Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohai_Sea).

In 2013, the pace of China's economic growth exceeded the domestic oil capacity and floods damaged the nation's oil fields in the middle of the year. Consequently, China imported oil to compensate for the supply reduction and surpassed the US in September 2013 to become the world's largest importer of oil.[^cite_note-252]

The total extent of China's [natural gas reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_reserves) is unknown, as relatively little exploration for natural gas has been done.[^cite_note-253] Sichuan accounts for almost half of the known natural gas reserves and production.[^cite_note-254] Most of the rest of China's natural gas is associated gas produced in the Northeast's major oil fields, especially [Daqing oilfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daqing_oilfield). Other gas deposits have been found in the Qaidam Basin, [Hebei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebei), [Jiangsu](166412.md), [Shanghai](27643.md), and [Zhejiang](96602.md), and offshore to the southwest of [Hainan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainan).[^cite_note-255] According to an article published in Energy Economics in 2011 by economists Mete Feridun (University of Greenwich) and Abdul Jalil (Wuhan University in China), financial development in China has not taken place at the expense of environmental pollution and financial development has led to a decrease in environmental pollution. Authors conclude that carbon emissions are mainly determined by income, energy consumption and trade openness and their findings confirm the existence of an Environmental Kuznets Curve in the case of China.[^cite_note-256]

#### Metals and nonmetals

See also: [Rare-earth industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rare-earth_industry_in_China), [History of metallurgy in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_metallurgy_in_China), and [Gold mining in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_mining_in_China)

A U.S.G.S. graph of global rare-earth-oxide production trends, 1956–2008

Global [rare-earth-oxide](145440.md) production trends, 1956–2008 ([USGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USGS))

[Iron ore](145352.md) reserves are found in most provinces, including [Hainan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainan). Gansu, Guizhou, southern Sichuan, and Guangdong provinces have rich deposits. The largest mined reserves are located north of the [Yangtze River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yangtze) and supply neighboring iron and steel enterprises. With the exception of [nickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel), [chromium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromium), and [cobalt](24580536.md), China is well supplied with [ferroalloys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferroalloy) and [manganese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manganese). Reserves of [tungsten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tungsten) are also known to be fairly large. [Copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper) resources are moderate, and high-quality ore is present only in a few deposits. Discoveries have been reported from [Ningxia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ningxia). [Lead](17747.md) and [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc) are available, and [bauxite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauxite) resources are thought to be plentiful. China's [antimony](898.md) reserves are the largest in the world. [Tin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tin) resources are plentiful, and there are fairly rich deposits of gold. China is the world's fifth largest producer of [gold](12240.md) and in the early 21st century became an important producer and exporter of rare metals needed in high-technology industries.

China also produces a fairly wide range of [nonmetallic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonmetal_\(chemistry\)) minerals. One of the most important of these is [salt](1605200.md), which is derived from coastal [evaporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evaporation) sites in Jiangsu, Hebei, Shandong, and Liaoning, as well as from extensive salt fields in Sichuan, Ningxia, and the Qaidam Basin. There are important deposits of [phosphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphate) rock in a number of areas, Jiangxi, Guangxi, Yunnan and Hubei. Production has been accelerating every year. As of 2013 China is producing 97,000,000 metric tons of phosphate rock a year.[^cite_note-257] [Pyrites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyrite) occur in several places; Liaoning, Hebei, Shandong, and Shanxi have the most important deposits. China also has large resources of [fluorite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluorite) ([fluorspar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluorspar)), [gypsum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gypsum), [asbestos](21492663.md), and has the world's largest reserves and production of [cement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cement), clinker and limestone.

#### Renewable energy

Main article: [Renewable energy in China](13556279.md)

Since the early 2000s, China's clean energy sector has rapidly developed.[^cite_note-Lewis_2023-258]<sup>: 23 </sup> This growth has enabled renewable energy to have an important role in China's international cooperation, including [South-South cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South-South_Cooperation) in which China is a major source of clean energy technology transfer to other developing countries.[^cite_note-Lewis_2023-258]<sup>: 4, 23 </sup> As of 2023, China is the world's leading producer of solar panels and wind turbines.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 167 </sup>

China has sought to lessen its reliance on importing fossil fuels. Fossil fuels still account for 80% of China's energy consumption, but renewables now generate 35% of its electricity and account for 83% of new electricity demand as of 2024. In a survey taken the same year, 44% of experts said that China's CO2 emissions would peak by the end of 2025, five years ahead of its official target. Chinese companies control 80% of the production chain in the world's green energy industry. Due to supply from green energy, China's industrial electricity prices have stabilized at \$0.09 per kilowatt hour for the past five years despite massive growth in electricity demand. Chinese renewable energy production has major international implications, with 87% of electricity production investment in the Global South going to the purchase of Chinese renewables and associated technologies.[^cite_note-259]

### Industry and manufacturing

Further information: [Industry of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_of_China), [Technological and industrial history of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technological_and_industrial_history_of_China), and [Made in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Made_in_China)



An industrial area in [Yishui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yishui_County), [Shandong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shandong_Province)



China Railway HXD1B Manufactured by [CRRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CRRC)



[Fuxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuxing_\(train\)) high speed train produced by [CRRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CRRC)



[C919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C919) developed by Chinese aerospace manufacturer [Comac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comac)

China has a strong global position in the production of industrial goods and some of its companies are global leader in the areas of steel, solar energy, and telecommunications accessories.[^cite_note-Sebena_2023-260]<sup>: 131 </sup> As of 2024, industry accounts for 36.5% of China's GDP.[^cite_note-NBS_Data-6] From 2010 until at least 2023, China produces more industrial goods than any other country.[^cite_note-Garlick_2024-108]<sup>: 1 </sup> As of 2023, China manufactures approximately one fifth of the world's total output of industrial products.[^cite_note-Sebena_2023-260]<sup>: 133 </sup>

Major industries include mining and ore processing; [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron) and [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel); [aluminium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aluminium); [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal); [machinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machinery); [armaments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armaments); [textiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textiles) and [apparel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apparel); [petroleum](23195.md); [cement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cement); [chemical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical); [fertilizers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertilizers); [food processing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_processing); [automobiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automobile) and other transportation equipment including rail cars and locomotives, ships, and aircraft; [consumer products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_products) including [footwear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Footwear), [toys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toy), and [electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics); [telecommunications](33094374.md) and [information technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_technology).

Since the founding of the People's Republic, industrial development has been given considerable attention; as of 2011 46% of China's national output continued to be devoted to investment; a percentage far higher than any other nation.[^cite_note-261] Among the various industrial branches the [machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine)-building and [metallurgical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallurgy) industries have received the highest priority. These two areas alone now account for about 20–30 percent of the total gross value of industrial output.[^cite_note-262] In these, as in most other areas of industry, however, [innovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innovation) has generally suffered at the hands of a system that has rewarded increases in gross output rather than improvements in variety, sophistication and quality. China, therefore, still imports significant quantities of specialized steels. Overall industrial output has grown at an average rate of more than ten percent per year, having surpassed all other sectors in [economic growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_growth) and degree of [modernization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernization).[^cite_note-263]

Following its 2001 entry into the World Trade Organization, China quickly developed a reputation as the "world's factory" through its manufacturing exports.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 256 </sup> The complexity of its exports increased over time, and as of 2019 it accounts for approximately 25% of all high tech goods produced globally.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 256 </sup> The proportion of Chinese manufactured goods at the higher end of the [value chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value_chain) grew at a faster rate after 2020.[^cite_note-\:2025MesaLago-184]<sup>: 38 </sup> As of 2024, China has significant industrial capacity in excess of its domestic needs.[^cite_note-Garlick_2024-108]<sup>: 34 </sup> The government has sought to alleviate industrial capacity by channeling it abroad, including through the [Belt and Road Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belt_and_Road_Initiative).[^cite_note-Garlick_2024-108]<sup>: 34 </sup>

The predominant focus of development in the [chemical industry](58721.md) is to expand the output of chemical [fertilizers](37401.md), [plastics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plastics), and [synthetic fibers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synthetic_fiber). The growth of this industry has placed China among the world's leading producers of [nitrogenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitrogenous) fertilizers. In the [consumer goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_goods) sector the main emphasis is on textiles and [clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clothing), which also form an important part of China's exports. Textile manufacturing, a rapidly growing proportion of which consists of synthetics, account for about ten percent of the gross industrial output and continues to be important, but less so than before. The industry tends to be scattered throughout the country, but there are a number of important [textile centers](51498947.md), including [Shanghai](27643.md), [Guangzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangzhou), and [Harbin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harbin).[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265] There is a [growing consumer culture in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growing_consumer_culture_of_China).

#### Steel industry

Main articles: [Steel industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_industry_in_China), [List of steel producers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_steel_producers), and [List of countries by steel production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_steel_production)



Skyscrapers of [Guangzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangzhou)

In 2020, China produced over 1053 million tonnes of steel, over half of the world total. This was an increase of 5.6% over the previous year as global steel production fell by 0.9%. China's share of global crude steel production increased from 53.3% in 2019 to 56.5% in 2020, decreasing -2.1% in 2021.[^cite_note-266]

[Iron ore](145352.md) production kept pace with steel production in the early 1990s but was soon outpaced by imported iron ore and other metals in the early 2000s. Steel production, an estimated 140 million tons in 2000 increased to 419 million tons in 2006 and 928 million tons by 2018. China was the top exporter of steel in the world in 2021; export volumes in 2021 were 66.2 million tons.[^cite_note-267] Domestic demand remains strong, particularly in the developing west where steel production in [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) was expanding.[^cite_note-268] Of the 45 largest steel producing companies in the world, 21 are Chinese, including the world's largest, [China Baowu Steel Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Baowu_Steel_Group).

#### Automotive industry

Main article: [Automotive industry in China](8518766.md)

China is the world's largest automobile producer, manufacturing more than 31.28 million vehicles in 2024, with 5.86 million of those being exported overseas.[^cite_note-269] As of 2024, China is the world's largest automobile market in terms of both sales and ownership.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 105 </sup> Due to rapid advancements by Chinese companies, China's automotive industry is regarded as one of the most competitive and innovative in the world.[^cite_note-\:37-270]

[Automobile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automobile) manufacturing has soared during the reform and opening up period. In 1975 only 139,800 automobiles were produced annually, but by 1985 production had reached 443,377, then jumped to nearly 1.1 million by 1992 and increased fairly evenly each year up until 2001, when it reached 2.3 million. By 2006 China had become the world's third largest automotive vehicle manufacturer and the second-largest consumer. By 2010, China was manufacturing more vehicles than the U.S. and Japan combined. Domestic sales have kept pace with production. After respectable annual increases in the mid- and late 1990s, passenger car sales soared in the early 2000s. In 2010, China became the world's largest automotive vehicle manufacturer as well as the largest consumer ahead of the [United States](3434750.md) with an estimated 18 million new cars sold.[^cite_note-271]

China began exporting car parts in 1999. China began to plan major moves into the automobile and components export business starting in 2005. By 2004, twelve major foreign automotive manufacturers had [joint-venture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint-venture) plants in China. They produced a wide range of automobiles, [minivans](20828.md), [sport utility vehicles](47696.md), [buses](4146.md), and [trucks](31456.md). In 2003, China exported US\$4.7 billion worth of vehicles and components. The vehicle export was 78,000 units in 2004, 173,000 units in 2005, and 340,000 units in 2006. China's exports of cars increased significantly since 2020, boosted by [new energy vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plug-in_electric_vehicles_in_China). It overtook Germany and Japan in 2022 and 2023 respectively in vehicle exports, becoming the world's largest exporter of cars.[^cite_note-272]

#### Cement industry

Main article: [Cement industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cement_industry_in_China)

This section is an excerpt from [Cement industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cement_industry_in_China).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Cement_industry_in_China\&action=edit)]

[China](5405.md) is the largest [cement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cement) producer in the world. As of 2004, it was responsible for 60% of cement global production. In 2017, China produced 2,400,000 metric tons of cement, whereas other countries produced about 1,728,300 metric tons.[^cite_note-Cement_industry_in_China_\:0-273]

Cement production adds one ton of [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide) (CO<sub>2</sub>) per ton of cement. According to engineering company [FLSmidth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FLSmidth), in order to heat a [cement kiln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cement_kiln) with waste, about three times more waste is needed than the amount of [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal) used, reducing emissions by only about 10%. However, the Chinese cement industry has improved [energy efficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efficient_energy_use). In 2017, it accounted for 57% of the global industry's output, but only 52% of its emissions. However, the emission reductions from energy efficiency measures are vague compared to those that could be achieved with innovative technologies such as [carbon capture and storage](3679268.md) (CCS), which sequesters emissions.

#### Advanced materials industry

Main article: [Advanced materials industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_materials_industry_in_China)

This section is an excerpt from [Advanced materials industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advanced_materials_industry_in_China).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Advanced_materials_industry_in_China\&action=edit)]

As an important part of the [national](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation) [sustainable development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_development) [strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategy), the development of [advanced materials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Materials_science) is advancing the [competitive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Competitive) nature and [state of the art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_the_art) for [Chinese industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_industry). The State has put advanced materials high on its development agenda for the next decade and listed it among the key [high-tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-tech) industry [sectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sector_\(economic\)) that would be given priority for development by the [State Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Council_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China). At present, the pace of building China's advanced materials industry is accelerating. Advanced materials have been key fields in China's national R\&D system (National High Technology Research and Development Program ([863 Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/863_Program)) and National Basic Research Program ([973 Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Program_973))).

#### Chemical industry

Main article: [Chemical industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_industry_in_China)

This section is an excerpt from [Chemical industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_industry_in_China).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Chemical_industry_in_China\&action=edit)]

&#x20;The chemical industry in the [People's Republic of China](5405.md) valued at around \$1.75 trillion in 2014.

[^cite_note-274]

&#x20;The country is currently the largest chemicals manufacturer in the world.

[^cite_note-275]



The chemical industry is central to modern China's economy. It uses special methods to alter the structure, composition or synthesis of substances to produce new products, such as steel, plastic, and ethyl. The chemical industry provides building materials for China's infrastructure, including subway, high-speed train, and highway.&#x20;

Prior to 1978, most of the products were produced by the state-owned enterprises, but the share in product output from state-owned business has since decreased.

The Chinese chemical industry is also one of the world's largest producers of both controlled and non-controlled [precursor chemicals](36201623.md) used in the Global [illicit drug trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illicit_drug_trade), particularly in the [Golden Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Triangle_\(Southeast_Asia\)), Mexico, Latin America and Europe,[^cite_note-Chemical_industry_in_China_\:4-276] with large volumes of these substances being traded through the growing research chemical (RC) industry online through social media and on B2B platforms and the dark web.[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278]

#### Defence and aerospace

Main pages: [Category\:Defence companies of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Defence_companies_of_China), [Category\:Aerospace companies of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Aerospace_companies_of_China), and [Chinese space program](343674.md)

China's defence industry is a rapidly advancing, state-driven sector, ranking as the top-four global arms exporter and focusing on high-tech aerospace, shipbuilding and missile systems. Fueled by massive, yet sometimes opaque investment nearing approximately US\$300–470 billion annually, it seeks technological superiority by 2027 while facing internal structural challenges, corruption and recent industry revenue declines. It is mainly dominated by state-owned enterprises (SOEs) such as [Norinco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norinco) (land systems), [AVIC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AVIC) (aircraft) and [China State Shipbuilding Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_State_Shipbuilding_Corporation) (CSSC).[^cite_note-279]

#### Electric vehicle industry

Main articles: [Electric vehicle industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_vehicle_industry_in_China) and [Plug-in electric vehicles in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plug-in_electric_vehicles_in_China)



&#x20;[BYD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BYD_Han) with Dual Mode hybrid engine

The electric vehicle industry in China is the largest in the world, accounting for around 58% of global consumption of EVs.[^cite_note-280] In 2023, CAAM reported China had sold 9.05 million passenger electric vehicles, consisting of 6.26 million BEVs (battery-only EVs) and 2.79 million PHEV (plug-in hybrid electric vehicles).[^cite_note-NE-281] China also dominates the plug-in [electric bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_bus) and [light commercial vehicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_commercial_vehicle) market, reaching over 500,000 buses (98% of global stock) and 247,500 electric commercial vehicles (65% of global stock) in 2019,[^cite_note-282] and recording new sales of 447,000 commercial EVs in 2023.[^cite_note-NE-281]

Plug-in electric vehicle (BEV and PHEV) sales were 37% of the overall automotive sales in China in 2023, with BEVs and PHEVs having 25% and 12% market share respectively. This is a significant increase from 2020, when plug-in electric vehicles accounted for only 6.3% of total sales.[^cite_note-283] The plug-in market in China was dominated by Chinese companies, with [BYD Auto](1461760.md) and [SAIC Motor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAIC_Motor) occupying the top two spots, and 5 out of the top 7 spots.[^cite_note-284]



Geographical distribution of the [global battery supply chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_vehicle_supply_chain) in 2024.

[^cite_note-285]

: 58&#x20;

The battery industry is closely related to the EV industry as [batteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_vehicle_battery) constitute around 1/3 of the cost of EVs[^cite_note-286] and around 80% of lithium-ion batteries in the world are used in EVs.[^cite_note-287] The industry also has significant Chinese presence, with major players including world's largest [CATL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CATL), [BYD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BYD_Company), CALB, Gotion, SVOLT and WeLion.[^cite_note-288]

#### Semiconductor industry

Main article: [Semiconductor industry in China](70425236.md)

The Chinese semiconductor industry, including [IC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_circuit) design and manufacturing, forms a major part of China's IT industry. China's semiconductor industry consists of a wide variety of companies, from [integrated device manufacturers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_device_manufacturer) to [pure-play foundries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor_fabrication_plant) to [fabless semiconductor companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabless_semiconductor_company). [Integrated device manufacturers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_device_manufacturer) (IDMs) design and manufacture integrated circuits. [Pure-play foundries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor_fabrication_plant) only manufacture devices for other companies, without designing them, while [fabless semiconductor companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabless_semiconductor_company) only design devices. Examples of Chinese IDMs are [YMTC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YMTC) and [CXMT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changxin_Memory_Technologies), examples of Chinese pure-play foundries are [SMIC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor_Manufacturing_International_Corporation), [Hua Hong Semiconductor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hua_Hong_Semiconductor) and [Wingtech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wingtech), and examples of Chinese fabless companies are [Zhaoxin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhaoxin), [HiSilicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HiSilicon) and [UNISOC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNISOC).

China is the currently the world's largest semiconductor market in terms of consumption. In 2020, China represented 53.7% of worldwide chip sales, or \$239.45 billion out of \$446.1 billion. However, a large percentage are imported from multinational suppliers. In 2020, imports took up 83.38% (\$199.7 billion) of total chip sales. In response, the country has launched a number of initiatives to close the gap, including investing \$150 billion into its domestic IC industry, with a "Made in China 2025" goal of 70% domestic production.[^cite_note-China_Accelerates-289][^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291] China leads the world in terms of number of new fabs under construction, with 8 out of 19 worldwide in 2021, and 17 fabs in total are expected to start construction from 2021 to 2023. Total installed capacity of Chinese-owned chipmakers will also increase from 2.96 million wafers per month (wpm) in 2020 to 3.572 million wpm in 2021.[^cite_note-China_Accelerates-289]

#### Electronics industry

Main article: [Electronics industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics_industry_in_China)

This section is an excerpt from [Electronics industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics_industry_in_China).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Electronics_industry_in_China\&action=edit)]

The [electronics industry in the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics_industry_in_China) grew rapidly after the [reform and opening up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up) under the national strategic policy of accelerating the "[informatization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informatization)" of its [industrial development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialization_in_China).[^cite_note-292] Subsequently, labour costs have risen and creating wealth for citizens. The industry has been a major contribution to the modernization of China and the development of new job opportunities. There are many instances of [labour exploitation](40067623.md) and subpar working conditions.[^cite_note-293]

In 2005, China's electronic information sector made up 16.6% of the country's [economic growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_growth) and its added-value output formed 7% of the [GDP](12594.md). [Manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing) was the sector that grew the fastest.[^cite_note-294]

As of 2011, China is the world's largest market for personal computers.[^cite_note-295]

Major Chinese electronics companies include [BOE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BOE_Technology_Group), [Changhong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changhong), [DJI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DJI_\(company\)), [Haier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haier), [Hisense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hisense), [Huawei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huawei), [Konka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konka_Group), [Lenovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenovo) (Hong Kong–based), [Meizu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meizu), [Oppo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oppo), [Panda Electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panda_Electronics), [Skyworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skyworth), [SVA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SVA_Group?action=edit\&redlink=1), [TCL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TCL_Corporation), [Vivo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vivo_\(technology_company\)), [Xiaomi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiaomi) and [ZTE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZTE).&#x20;

China's production recorded the largest world market share for its electronics exports in 2016. It also recorded high volume outputs across a wide spectrum of [consumer electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_electronics); between 2014 and 2015—according to China Daily—286.2 million personal computers (90.6% of the global supply), 1.77 billion phones (70.6% of global supply of smartphones) and 109 million units (80% of global supply of air conditioners) were produced.[^cite_note-296]

#### Pharmaceutical industry

Main article: [Pharmaceutical industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutical_industry_in_China)

This section is an excerpt from [Pharmaceutical industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutical_industry_in_China).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Pharmaceutical_industry_in_China\&action=edit)]

|  | This article needs to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(November 2016)* |
| --- | --- |

The pharmaceutical industry is one of the leading industries in the [People's Republic of China](5405.md), covering synthetic chemicals and drugs, prepared Chinese medicines, medical devices, apparatus and instruments, hygiene materials, packing materials, and [pharmaceutical machinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutics). China has the second-largest pharmaceutical market in the world as of 2017 which is worth US\$110 billion.[^cite_note-297] China accounts for 20% of the world's population but only a small fraction of the global [drug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug) market. China's [changing health-care environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_system_reform_in_the_People's_Republic_of_China) is designed to extend basic [health insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance) to a larger portion of the population and give individuals greater access to products and services. Following the period of change, the pharmaceutical industry is expected to continue its expansion.[^cite_note-298]

China, as of 2007, has around 3,000 to 6,000 domestic pharmaceutical manufacturers and around 14,000 domestic pharmaceutical distributors. The most often-cited adverse factors in the marketplace include a lack of protection of [intellectual property rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intellectual_property_rights), a lack of visibility for drug approval procedures, a lack of effective governmental oversight, poor corporate support for drug research, and differences in the [treatment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Therapeutics) in China that are accorded to local and foreign firms.[^cite_note-Pharmaceutical_industry_in_China_unknownworldwatch-299]

Research and development are increasing, with Shanghai becoming one of the most important global drug research centers. Most notably, [Novartis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novartis) is expected to establish a large Research and development base in [Shanghai](27643.md) that will be a pillar of its drug development.[^cite_note-Pharmaceutical_industry_in_China_dyerft2006-300]

#### Shipping industry

Main articles: [Shipping industry of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipping_industry_of_China) and [Container industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Container_industry_in_China)

This section is an excerpt from [Shipping industry of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipping_industry_of_China).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Shipping_industry_of_China\&action=edit)]



A COSCO container ship sails from [Boston Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Harbor).

In 1961 [China](5405.md) established a state-run maritime shipping company and subsequently signed shipping agreements with many countries, laying the foundation for developing the country's ocean transport. That organization developed into the present-day China Ocean Shipping (Group) Company ([COSCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COSCO)). The Chinese government also invested heavily in water transport infrastructure, constructing new ports and rebuilding and enlarging older facilities.

A major effort has also been made to increase [mechanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanization) and [containerization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containerization) at major international ports.

China's [shipping industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipping_industry) and [container transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Container_transportation) have reached international standards both in handling efficiency and building networks.

The governmental responsibility of the shipping industry is under the [Ministry of Transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Transport_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China).

The number of container units handled by Chinese ports in 2011 reached more than 150 million. The country also manufactures 90% of the world's containers.

The throughput of cargo and containers at China's ports has been the largest in the world for the past five years, with an annual growth rate of 35%.

#### Other industries

See also: [Electric motor manufacturing industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_motor_manufacturing_industry_in_China), [Railway equipment industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railway_equipment_industry_in_China), and [Sex toy industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_toy_industry_in_China)

Substantial investments were made in the manufacture of [solar panels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_panel) and [wind generators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_generator) by a number of companies, supported by liberal loans by banks and local governments. However, by 2012 manufacturing capacity had far outstripped domestic and global demand for both products, particularly solar panels, which were subjected to anti-dumping penalties by both the United States and Europe. The global oversupply has resulted in bankruptcies and production cutbacks both inside and outside China. China has budgeted \$50 billion to subsidize production of solar power over the two decades following 2015.[^cite_note-301]



[Huawei MateBook series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huawei_MateBook_series) at a World Mobile Congress

China is the world's biggest sex toy producer and accounts for 70% of the worldwide sex toys production.[^cite_note-China_Daily_2010-302] In the country, 1,000 manufacturers are active in this industry, which generates about two billion dollars a year.[^cite_note-China_Daily_2010-302] As of 2011, China was the world's largest market for personal computers.[^cite_note-303] China has the second-largest reserve of computers in the world as of 2024.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 16–17 </sup>

### Services

Main page: [Category\:Service industries in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Service_industries_in_China)

Prior to the onset of economic reforms in 1978, China's services sector was characterized by state-operated [shops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retailing), [rationing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationing), and regulated prices – with reform came private markets, individual entrepreneurs, and a commercial sector. The [wholesale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wholesale) and [retail](183515.md) trade has expanded quickly, with numerous [shopping malls](235723.md), retail shops, restaurant chains and hotels constructed in urban areas. [Public administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_administration) remains a main component of the service sector, while [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_China) has become a significant factor in [employment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment) and a source of [foreign exchange](1015869.md).[^cite_note-304]

#### Telecommunications

Main articles: [Telecommunications in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_China) and [Telecommunications industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_industry_in_China)

See also: [Mobile phone industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_phone_industry_in_China), [Internet in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_in_China), and [Software industry in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Software_industry_in_China)

The affordability of [mobile phones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_phone_industry_in_China) and [internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_in_China) data in China has resulted in the number of mobile internet users in China surpassing the number of computer internet users.[^cite_note-305]<sup>: 178 </sup> By 2023, the number of Internet users in China increased to over 1.09 billion. The proportions of Chinese netizens accessing the Internet via mobile phones, desktop computers, laptop computers, TVs and tablet computers were 99.9%, 33.9%, 30.3%, 22.5% and 26.6%, respectively.[^cite_note-306]

#### Business process outsourcing in China

Main article: [Business process outsourcing in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_process_outsourcing_in_China)

#### Consumer retailing

Main pages: [Category\:Retailing in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Retailing_in_China) and [Category\:Chinese brands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Chinese_brands)

See also: [E-commerce in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E-commerce_in_China) and [Online advertising in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online_advertising_in_China)

China's economy is one of the world's leaders in consumer retailing, consumer internet and [mobile payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobile_payment).[^cite_note-Sebena_2023-260]<sup>: 130 </sup> As of 2024, China has more [internet users](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netizen) than any other country.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 18 </sup> Internet users in China generate large amounts of data, thereby providing a competitive benefit in the development of machine learning for artificial intelligence technology.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 18 </sup> Mobile payment methods via apps including [Alipay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alipay) and [WeChat Pay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WeChat_Pay) were quickly adopted in China in part due to the relative lack of credit cards in the country.[^cite_note-Sebena_2023-260]<sup>: 130 </sup> This technological [leapfrogging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leapfrogging) also led to a boom in online shopping and retail banking.[^cite_note-Sebena_2023-260]<sup>: 130 </sup>

#### Platform economy

China's platform economy has grown substantially since the early 2010s, with its transactional volume reaching RMB 3.7 trillion in 2021.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 155 </sup> The platform economy has absorbed a large number of workers from China's decreasing manufacturing workforce and from its population of [internal migrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migration_in_China) workers.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 155 </sup> As of 2020, 84 million people worked as platform economy service providers and 6 million were employees of platform companies.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 155 </sup>

The platform economy sector is highly concentrated in [Alibaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alibaba_Group) and [Tencent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tencent), both of which have ride-hailing and food delivery businesses as key assets in their investment portfolios.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 155 </sup> Ride-hailing and food delivery businesses have significantly consolidated since 2016.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 155–156 </sup>

*The New York Times* described both Alibaba and Tencent as China's two dominant internet giants competing to control daily life through e-commerce, payments, and social media.[^cite_note-307] In mobile payments, many companies have to partner with Alibaba's Alipay or Tencent's platform services. They also control major apps and payment platforms, which limits competition in the digital space. Both Alibaba and Tencent are recognized as tech giants that are driving innovation in e-commerce, social networking, and mobile services. Alibaba generates the majority of its revenue from e-commerce platforms like Taobao and Tmall, while Tencent derives most of its revenue from social services like WeChat, gaming, and fintech.[^cite_note-308]

#### Education

Main article: [Education in China](407843.md)

This section is an excerpt from [Education in China](407843.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Education_in_China\&action=edit)]



Students in [Tieling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tieling)

[Education in the People's Republic of China](407843.md) is primarily managed by the state-run [public education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_education) system, which falls under the [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(China\)). By law, education in the [People's Republic of China](5405.md) is led by the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) (CCP) and adheres to [its ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party). All citizens must attend school for a minimum of nine years, known as nine-year [compulsory education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compulsory_education), which is funded by the government through the national education budget.

[Compulsory education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compulsory_education_in_China) includes six years of [elementary school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_education), typically starting at the age of six and finishing at the age of twelve,[^cite_note-309] followed by three years of [middle school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_school). A middle school graduate may choose to continue their secondary education for three more years in a [high school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_education) or a [vocational school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocational_school).

In 2020, the Ministry of Education reported an increase of new entrants of 34.4 million students entering compulsory education, bringing the total number of students who attend compulsory education to 156 million.[^cite_note-310]

In 1985, the government abolished tax-funded [higher education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education), requiring university applicants to compete for [scholarships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholarship) based on their academic capabilities. In the early 1980s, the government allowed the establishment of the first [private institution of higher learning](612857.md), thus increasing the number of [undergraduates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undergraduate) and people who hold [doctoral degrees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctoral_degree) from 1995 to 2005.[^cite_note-311]

Chinese investment in research and development has grown by 20 percent per year since 1999, exceeding \$100 billion in 2011. As many as 1.5 million science and engineering students graduated from Chinese universities in 2006. By 2008, China had published 184,080 papers in recognized international journals – a seven-fold increase from 1996.[^cite_note-312] In 2017, China surpassed the U.S. with the highest number of scientific publications.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314] In 2021, there were 3,012 universities and colleges (see [List of universities in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_China)) in China,[^cite_note-315] and 147 [National Key Universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Key_Universities), which are considered to be part of an elite group [Double First Class universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_First-Class_Construction), accounted for approximately 4.6% of all higher education institutions in China.[^cite_note-316]

China has also been a top destination for international students and as of 2013, China was the most popular country in Asia for international students and ranked third overall among countries.[^cite_note-Education_in_China_USNEWS-317] China is now the leading destination globally for Anglophone African students[^cite_note-318] and is host of the second largest international students population in the world.[^cite_note-319] As of 2025, there were 2 Chinese universities in the global top 20, 5 in the top 50, and 19 in the top 200, behind only the United States and the United Kingdom in terms of the overall representation in the Aggregate Ranking of Top Universities, a composite ranking system combining three of the world's most influential university rankings ([ARWU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_Ranking_of_World_Universities)+[QS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QS_World_University_Rankings)+ [THE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Times_Higher_Education_World_University_Rankings)).[^cite_note-Education_in_China_Results_ARTU-320]

Chinese students in the country's most developed regions are among the best performing in the world in the [Programme for International Student Assessment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) (PISA).[^cite_note-Education_in_China_\:05-321]<sup>: 142 </sup> [Shanghai](27643.md), [Beijing](18603746.md), [Jiangsu](166412.md) and [Zhejiang](96602.md) outperformed all other education systems in the PISA.[^cite_note-322] According to estimates from *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* in 2026, China would have scored among the highest even if every single province had taken part in the test. For example, the country's math score would have been 536, the fourth highest in the world, while its average score across all three subjects would have been 501, the seventh highest in the world.[^cite_note-Education_in_China_\:3-323] China's educational system has been noted for its emphasis on rote memorization and test preparation. However, PISA spokesman [Andreas Schleicher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Schleicher) says that China has moved away from learning by rote in recent years.[^cite_note-324] According to Schleicher, Russia performs well in rote-based assessments, but not in PISA, whereas China does well in both rote-based and broader assessments.[^cite_note-Education_in_China_ft-1-325]

#### Healthcare

Main article: [Healthcare in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_China)

This section is an excerpt from [Healthcare in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_China).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Healthcare_in_China\&action=edit)]

[Healthcare in the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_China) is primarily provided by [state-owned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_ownership) hospitals, with [medical insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance) administered primarily by [local governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government). As of 2020, about 95% of the population has at least basic [health insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_in_China) coverage.[^cite_note-Healthcare_in_China_\:8-326]

Medical insurances are subdivided into employee and resident insurance. The former covers the urban employed population, and the latter covers the urban non-employed population and the rural population. A total of 25% of the people covered by the basic medical insurance participated in the employee medical insurance, a total of 344 million people; 75% participated in the residents' medical insurance, a total of 1.017 billion people.[^cite_note-Healthcare_in_China_\:8-326] Medical assistance has subsidized 78 million poor people to participate in basic medical insurance, and the coverage of poor people has stabilized at over 99.9%.[^cite_note-Healthcare_in_China_\:8-326]

Despite this, public health insurance generally only covers about half of medical costs, with the proportion lower for serious or [chronic illnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronic_condition). Under the "Healthy China 2020" initiative, China undertook an effort to cut healthcare costs, requiring insurance to cover 70% of costs by the end of 2018.[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-Healthcare_in_China_nytimes.com-328] In addition, there are policies such as [critical illness insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_illness_insurance) and medical assistance. China's commercial health insurance is also proliferating. In 2020, the country's commercial health insurance premium income amounted to 817.3 billion yuan, with an average annual growth rate of 20%. China's coverage of maternity insurance has continued to expand, by the end of 2020, 235.673 million people were insured under maternity insurance.[^cite_note-Healthcare_in_China_\:8-326]

The country maintains two parallel medical systems, one for modern or Western medicine, and one for [Traditional Chinese medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_medicine) (TCM). Some Chinese consider TCM backward and ineffective, others consider it inexpensive, effective, and culturally appropriate. China has also become a major market for health-related multinational companies. Companies such as [AstraZeneca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AstraZeneca), [GlaxoSmithKline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GSK_plc), [Eli Lilly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eli_Lilly_and_Company), and [Merck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merck_Group) entered the Chinese market and have experienced explosive growth. China has also become a growing hub for healthcare research and development.[^cite_note-Healthcare_in_China_McKinsey-329] According to [Sam Radwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Radwan) of [ENHANCE International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ENHANCE_International), China's projected healthcare spending in 2050 may exceed Germany's entire 2020 gross domestic product.[^cite_note-Healthcare_in_China_Enhance-330]

The [special administrative regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_administrative_regions_of_China) of Hong Kong and [Macau](19068.md) maintain their own separate universal healthcare systems.[^cite_note-331]

#### Mass media

Main article: [Mass media in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_China)

See also: [Video games in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_games_in_China)

In 2020, China's market for [Chinese films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_China) surpassed the U.S. market to become the largest such market in the world.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 16 </sup> Domestic movies dominate the Chinese market.[^cite_note-332]

This section is an excerpt from [Video games in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_games_in_China).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Video_games_in_China\&action=edit)]



Games market of China by revenue per platform in 2015

[^cite_note-333]

The video game industry in [China](5405.md) currently is one of the major markets for the global [video game industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_game_industry), where more than half a billion people play video games. Revenues from [China](5405.md) make up around 25% of the nearly US\$100 billion global video game industry as of 2018. Since 2015, China has exceeded the contribution to the global market from the [United States](3434750.md).[^cite_note-334] Because of its market size, China has been described as the "Games Industry Capital of the World" and is home to some of the largest video game companies.[^cite_note-335] China has also been a major factor in the growth of [esports](564204.md), both in player talent and in revenue.

#### Tourism

Main articles: [Tourism in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_China) and [List of World Heritage Sites in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_China)



A guide leads a group of Norwegian tourists to the [Temple of Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Heaven) in [Beijing](18603746.md)

China hosts the world's second [largest number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Sites_by_country#Countries_with_major_concentrations_of_World_Heritage_Sites) of [World Heritage Sites](44940.md) ([59](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_China)). China's [tourism industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_industry) is one of the fastest-growing industries in the national economy and is also one of the industries with a very distinct global competitive edge. According to the [World Travel and Tourism Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Travel_and_Tourism_Council), travel and tourism directly contributed CNY 1,362 billion (US\$216 billion) to the Chinese economy (about 2.6% of GDP).[^cite_note-336] In 2011, total international tourist arrivals was 58 million, and international tourism receipts were US\$48 billion.[^cite_note-337]

The Chinese tourism industry was hit hard by both the [COVID-19 lockdowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdowns) and strained relations with many foreign nations.[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339] Foreign flights into China, both for business and tourism are way down, especially from the US.[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342] Recent much increased internal security activity decreases the desire of foreigners to live in China, or to go to China, for fear of being targeted and not allowed to leave. Thus many foreigners are leaving and others crossing it off as a travel destination.[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344]

#### Luxury goods

Main article: [Luxury goods of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxury_goods_of_China)

Hong Kong and Macau benefit from favorable taxation rules and are favored locations for tourists from elsewhere in China to purchase luxury goods like cosmetics, jewelry, and designer fashion goods.[^cite_note-Simpson_2023-345]<sup>: 116 </sup> [Porcelain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porcelain) has long been one of China's most important luxury exports.[^cite_note-Simpson_2023-345]<sup>: 226 </sup> It was especially important to early trade between China and the West, with much of that trade being conducted through Macau.[^cite_note-Simpson_2023-345]<sup>: 226 </sup> Many shops in international travel destinations have specialized staff devoted to Chinese customers.[^cite_note-Yunxu_2013-346]

## Informal economy

Main article: [Informal economy of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_economy_of_China)

See also: [Gambling in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gambling_in_China) and [Prostitution in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prostitution_in_China)

This section is an excerpt from [Informal economy of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_economy_of_China).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Informal_economy_of_China\&action=edit)]

|  | This article needs to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(April 2026)* |
| --- | --- |

The [informal economy of the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_economy_of_China) refers to a range of informal economic activity that stands outside of the recognized systems of regulations, taxation, and licenses. Although the term is sometimes understood to describe marginal, unregulated, and even criminal activity, there is considerable overlap between the [informal sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_sector) and formal economy, and the informal economy serves an important societal function in contributing to employment and economic growth.

The informal economy in China represents a large portion of domestic output, consumption and employment. Employment in the urban sector represents a major part of the Chinese economy: approximately half of urban Chinese workers belong to the informal economy as of 2004.[^cite_note-Informal_economy_of_China_OCDE-347]—a significant increase since the 1990s. It is described as fast-growing, dynamic, highly competitive, and it contributes substantially to economic growth.[^cite_note-Informal_economy_of_China_OCDE-347] However, there are serious concerns about the lack of protections afforded to workers in the informal sector.

In addition, China is a major part of the global informal sector, producing unregulated goods for consumption in Africa, Latin America, and elsewhere.[^cite_note-348] According to Roberta Neuwirth, China's rise as a global center of manufacturing owes, in part, to its willingness to trade in the informal economy, also known as [system D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/System_D) trade.[^cite_note-Informal_economy_of_China_Stealth-349] In some regions in China, local governments have adopted policies designed to encourage the informal economy to alleviate unemployment.[^cite_note-Informal_economy_of_China_Sida-350]

## Income and wealth

Main article: [Income distribution in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_distribution_in_China)

See also: [List of Chinese provincial-level divisions by GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chinese_provincial-level_divisions_by_GDP), [List of Chinese provincial-level divisions by GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chinese_provincial-level_divisions_by_GDP_per_capita), [List of top Chinese cities by GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_top_Chinese_cities_by_GDP), and [List of prefecture-level divisions of China by GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_prefecture-level_divisions_of_China_by_GDP)

China has the largest national proportion of the global middle class.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 204 </sup> As of 2020, China had 400 million middle-income citizens.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 159 </sup> It is projected to reach 1.2 billion by 2027, making up one fourth of the world total.[^cite_note-351] According to a 2021 [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) survey, there were 23 million Chinese individuals with a per capita daily income of \$50 or more, 242 million with a daily income between \$20–50 per day, 493 million between \$10–20, 641 million \$2–10 per day, and 4 million under \$2 per day; all the figures are expressed in [international dollars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_dollar) and 2011 purchasing power parity values.[^cite_note-352] In 2022, the [National Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bureau_of_Statistics_of_China) reported that China's average disposable income per capita was ¥36,883, of which ¥20,590 was from wages and salaries, ¥6,175 was net business income, ¥3,227 was net income from property, and ¥6,892 was net transfer income.[^cite_note-353] The [median per capita disposable income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_income) was reported as ¥36,231 with a 4.4% annual growth for 2025.[^cite_note-x425-354]

As of April 2023, China was second in the world, after the US, in [total number of billionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_billionaires) and [total number of millionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_millionaires), with 495 Chinese billionaires[^cite_note-355] and 6.2 million millionaires in 2022.[^cite_note-356] According to the Hurun Global Rich Report In 2020, China had the world's highest number of billionaires, which is more than the US and India combined,[^cite_note-357] and as of March 2021, the number of billionaires in China reach 1,058 with the combined wealth of US\$4.5 trillion.[^cite_note-Hurun_2021-358] According to the 2019 Global Wealth Report by Credit Suisse Group, China surpassed the US in the wealth of the top ten percent of the world's population.[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360] In other words, as of 2019, a hundred million Chinese are in the top ten percent of the wealthiest individuals in the world – those who have a net personal wealth of at least \$110,000.[^cite_note-361] According to the list in 2021, China is home to six of the world's top ten cities (Beijing, Shanghai, Shenzhen, Hong Kong, [Hangzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hangzhou) and [Guangzhou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangzhou) in the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 5th, 8th and 9th spots, respectively) by the highest number of billionaires, which is more than any other country.[^cite_note-Hurun_2021-358] As of January 2021, China had 85 female billionaires, two-thirds of the global total.[^cite_note-362]

The average income of a rural resident in China is 30% of the average income for an urban resident.[^cite_note-Li_2024-107]<sup>: 191 </sup> The rural population is relatively older as the rural youth migrate to cities for higher earning jobs while the aged stay behind. Much of this rural population works small plots of land in order to survive and have little earning power. Former Chinese Premier Li Keqiang stated, in 2020, that 600 million Chinese live on or under 1,000 yuan per month.[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364] At a conversion rate of 7 yuan to the dollar, this translates to \$143 per month. A family of 3 at this income level would have a yearly income of \$5,150. The difference in income is also a reflection of the difference in retirement payments to urban retirees versus rural retirees. Urban workers receive much more a month in retirement benefits on average compared to rural/farm workers who receive a pittance.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] Despite income inequality in China, the absolute income of nearly all income groups has risen quickly.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 204 </sup> From 1988 to 2018, China's rural and urban populations had per capita increases in [real income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_income) (i.e., accounting for inflation) of 8–10 times.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 204 </sup>

China's economic growth led to a major decrease in world inequality.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 202 </sup> Since China's reform and opening up, more than 1 billion Chinese people have been lifted out of poverty.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 202 </sup> The majority of global poverty reduction between 1981 and 2008 occurred in China.[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 202 </sup> As academic Lan Xiaohuan writes, during that period, "the number of poor people in the world outside China remained more or less unchanged. It can therefore be concluded that achievements in global poverty reduction come mainly from China."[^cite_note-AI-109]<sup>: 202–203 </sup> The historical consensus is that the policies of the post-[Mao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao) era significantly reduced [poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_China).[^cite_note-367] Nevertheless, under Mao, according to Sullivan et al., "China's government provided food and shelter at little or no cost. This meant US\$1.90 was able to buy more basic necessities in China than in comparable capitalist countries."[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369]

In terms of domestic [saving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_saving), defined as combined saving of households, businesses, and governments, China is the world's largest saving country as of 2022.[^cite_note-370]<sup>: 27 </sup>

### Wages

See also: [Minimum wage in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage_in_China)

In 1979–1980, the state reformed factories by giving wage increases to workers, which was immediately offset by sharply rising [inflation](38286.md) rates of 6–7%. Urban wages rose rapidly from 2004 to 2007, at a rate of 13 to 19% per year with average wages near \$200/month in 2007.[^cite_note-371] By 2016 the average monthly wage for workers engaged in manufacturing goods for export was \$424. This wage, combined with other costs of doing business in China, had, more or less, equalized any Chinese cost advantage with respect to developed economies.[^cite_note-372]

### Taxes

Main article: [Taxation in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_China)

The government's tax revenues primarily result from indirect taxes like the value added tax.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 6 </sup> [China's personal income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax_in_China) accounts for about 6.5% of tax revenues, as of 2024.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 6 </sup> It is a [progressive tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_tax), with the top income tax bracket is taxed at 45% of income.[^cite_note-Zhang-171]<sup>: 6 </sup>

### Inequality

Main article: [Income inequality in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_inequality_in_China)

## External trade

Main article: [History of trade of the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_trade_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China)

Further information: [List of the largest trading partners of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_largest_trading_partners_of_China), [List of exports of China](46538605.md), [List of Chinese administrative divisions by exports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chinese_administrative_divisions_by_exports), and [Free trade agreements of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade_agreements_of_China)

Treemap of product exports from China in 2019.
